PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"TRUST men and they will trust you," said Emerson. "Trust men and they will bast you," says the average business man. "THE pleasantest way to take cod-liver oil," Bays an old gourmand, "is to fatten pigeons with it, and then eat the pigeons." A PERMANENT ATTRACTION.—CIara : "Are you not afraid that some one will marry you for your money ?" Dora: "I would rather be married for money than for beauty." "Of all things! , Why ? ' "Beauty fades, but money can be kept at interest." LASTED A LONG TlME.—Willis : "When my wife makgs me a present it is sure to be something that will last." Wallace : "My wife is just like her. Five years ago she made me a present of a hundred cigars, and I have ninety-nine of them' yet." MEN never take their wives fishing with them if they can help it. There are so few women who will go fishing with their husbands, and agree to every thing the husbands tell when they come back

SHE WAS TIRED.—" Well, may I hope, then, ded.redt, that at some time I may have the happi- ness of making you my wife V "Yes, I hope so, .I am sure," she replied. "I am tired of suing fellows for breach of promise." DISSIPATION. —" I don't knew what I'm going to do about my husband," said the owl, with a sob. "What's the master ?" asked the sympathetic

nightingale. "His habits are getting io lie some- what dreadful. This is the second time this week that he has been up all day.' DIFFICULT, BUT SATISFACTORY. Mamma: "What are you trying to draw V' 'Little Ethel: "A elephant." "Rather a difficult subject." "I'd rather draw elephants than anything else, because my friends can always tell what it is. They knows a elephant is the only animal wif two

tails." "PAPA," said Willie, "I wish you'd tell me. If you were Queen Victoria and mamma was the Emperor of China-- Got that ?" "Yes." "Well, if you was they, who I'd be?" MARRIED MAN: "Why don't you get married, Miss Perkins? You are getting to look like a back number '—you will soon be an old maid." Miss Perkins: "If I were as easy to please as your wife was, I would have been married long ago. MOTHER: "When do you suppose that young man who calls upon you will make known his in- tentions, Laura ?" Laura: "I think he will pro- posd pretty soon. Last evening he was very anxious to know whether I could dress on £2O a year." PRETTY WIFE: "I knew you'd like this new hat, darling. It is becoming, and nothing could be simpler, could it!" Darling (thoughtfully) : "Nothing, except .the man who has to pay three

guineas for it." GROWING YOUNG AGAIN.--TWO ladies discovered talking in a corner of the drawing-room : "Had not Mrs. M— grey hair last year ? " "Yes." "How does it happen to be black now ? " You forget, my dear, that she has lost her husband since then." AUNT : "I want to do something to please you on yourbirthday, Charlie; but first I want to ask your teacher how you behave in school." Charlie: "If you really wish to please me, auntie, don't ask him." PEOPLE who wonder why men's hair turns grey before their whiskers should remember that there is about twenty years' difference in their respective ages. WHMI WE FEEL SMALL.—" There are times," said the man with the oratorical manner, "when we are overwhelmed with humiliation at the powerlessness of the human mind." "That's very true," was the reply. "I am 'often made to feel so." "Indeed ?" Yes. I have a four-year-old daughter who asks questions." CRUELTY TO AN INFANT.—" HOW did it happen that BLgs was arrested by an agent for the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children ?" "He was caught in the act of abusing his baby." "What was he doing ?" "Singing it to sleep.' "I SAW your name in print the other day," said one man to another, who was very fond of notoriety. "Where ?" asked the other, in pleased excitement. "In the directory Lovima MOTHER : "I cannot understand what makes our boy, Robert, so fond of pedestrianism." Fond Father: ." He gets that froin me. Didn't I walk the floor with him for weeks when he was a baby." " THREE kinds of juries figure prominently in trials now," remarked McCorckle. "What are they ?" asked McCradkle. "Grand jury, common jury, and perjury." BOTH SATISFIED. —Estella "What a lovely ball! I never enjoyed myself so much in all my life." Gladys "Nor I." : "I danced every dance, and had a different partner each time." Gladys: "I sat out every dance, and had the same partner all the evening ! ' Miss Arrioue (school teacher) : "What does w-h-i-t-e spell ?" Class—no answer. Miss An. : "What is the colour of my skin?' Class (in chorus) : "Yellow." "Few people," said the wife 4 as she proceeded to investigate her husband's pockets after he had gone to sleep—"few people are aware of what a wife has to go through." A SPOILED PUBLIC. —Boy: "Paper, sir Up- todate : "Well, I don't know. Have you any paper that prints coupons which you can take to the office next day and exchange for a fresh

paper ?" GOLDRICH : "I've fixed it so that there will be no contest over my will." Silvercash : "Impos- sible." Goldrich : "Not at aIL I've left every- thing to the lawyers." A LABOUR PESSIMIST.—Agitator "Oi've bees all over this town, and it's goin' to the dogs; that's phwat it is." Mechanic: "What's wrong with, it ?" Agitator: "Oi can't get up a stroike any- where." SPOKE FROM EXPERlENCE.—Temperance Orator: "Even an innocent baby has a wonderful influence over a man." Man on Back Seat: "RighT. you are, stranger. That's what drove me to drink." NEVER bear more than one kind of trouble at a time. Some people bear three kinds—all they have had, all they have now, and all they expect to ha. e. A FARMER saw a recipe advertised for keeping wells and cisterns from freezing in winter. Having sent a dozen stamps he received the following: "Take in your well or cistern at night, and stand it in front of the fire.'' A "LORD OF CREATION. "—A hen-pecked hus- band called the servant maid aside, said said: "Look here, Robustina, I am told that my wife and daughteri, are planning a trip to Biarritz; do you know whether I sin going with them or not?" APARTMENTS TO LET.—We cull the following advertisement from a German paper: "Furnished apartments for a gentleman 12 feet long and 10 feet wide. PRiscILLA : "They say that Clara's hair is not her own." Prunella : "Do they? What a shame ! Why, she showed me a receipted bill for it the other day." • " Was your watchman well recommended ?" "Oh, not directly. I used to see him in church, and as lie stayed awake all through Dr. Sonora's sermons, I concluded he was the manl wanted," "YES, sir," said Jenkins; " Sinithers is a man who keeps his word ; but then 11.- has to." "How is that r asked Johnson. "Because no one will take it." •SHOPPING IN THE COUNTRY.—" No, ma.am ; those are two articles we don't kee- ; but the oysters, I think, you will find at the post-office, and the onions you can get aOross the way, at the barber's.' " Flow are you finding business' doctor ?" was asked of a physician. "Capital,' he replied; "I have all I can attend to." "I didn't understand that there was much illness about." "No, there isn't. But we physicians do not depend upon sicxness for an ineo ipe. Oh, no; most of our money is made from people who have nothing the matter with them." LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

I gave you some hints about evening dresses last week, but I am sure you will not object if I allude to the subject again. You are sure to want a new one if you are going out much. A smart little costume is of a delightful shade of old-rose pink spotted silk, with trimmings of string-oo!oured lace, and ribbon to match or a contrast. The skirt Is trimmed at the foot with a soalloped frill of lace headed with a twist of ribbon, and a smart bow of the same at the top of each scallop. The round- waisted bodice is stretched over the tight-fitting lining, the material arranged in a few pleats at the centre of the waist both back and front, where the bodice fastens with hooks, the back being out without seams. The neck is out rounded at the back and V-shape in front, and is finished with a narrow lace edging. A full benne 'of lace is attached to the edge of the bodice. Ribbon starts from beneath a bow at the bust, passes under the arms, and crosses at the back, and is brought round again to the front, where it is tied in a bow at the side. The balloon sleeves are made up over a lining foundation, and are surmounted by lace epaulettes. Another, and this a most handsome dress, is of primrose brocade, veiled with white areophane. The drapery round the plain bodice of primrose-coloured poult de soie is of broderie Apglaise. Parisians, lam told, are much &fleabag the swallow-tail out of which this bodice is an example. The neck is out low, with a V-shaped opening at the back.

A great deal of pleasure is deriveci from dressing ' up for children's fancy dress balls, and I cannot do better than give you some ideas as to. 'costumes for these delightful functions. No matter • how pretty the ordinary party frock, it, bears no ()gm- ,parison with the fancy- costume, and the idea of even for a few hours representing some other character, has a peculiar fascination fox children. Simplebut piquant costumes can be easily Made at a slight expense, and only ordinary ma- terials need be employed. Tske, for instance, a very fair child with fluffy golden hair. This can be lightly powered, and patches nu, on the chin and neck. A dainty little Watteau costume of white sateen, che.ap_ white lace, whit, roses, and satin ribbons will make a delightfw "Dresden China ". or "Watteau Blanche." F.- the dark-haired damsel with rogui:th eyes, a gips); costume of red merino, black sateen, and yellow Pongee can be embellished with large gold sequins, a littlet gold braid, and a tambourine with multi- coloured ribbons. Or with a short velvet boclie, laced over a chemieette of nainsook muslin, and a skirt of royal blue nun's cloth, trimmed with velvet bands, she may represent an Italian peasant, using one of the Algerian tinselled antimacassars now sold at 63. and Bd. each, for an apron, and for the headdress a square of cardboard about five inches each way, and covered with one half of anotner antimacassar, or with a fancy square over a white cambric pugaree.

The following costumes are beautifslly simple, and may be easily made at home. The first is that of a fairy, and makes a charming dress for a little maiden about six. The dress may be made of pink materials, and the skirt is extended. Wings and stockings should be of the same colours. A star on the top of the head and a little sceptre completes the get-up. The Queen of Hears is another effective costume ; the wearer carries a sceptre, and her dress is dotted with large crime on hearts. A lovely dress is that of a Pacha's daughter. There are full trousers of rose-coloured silk, with a tunic and sleeves of broussa gauze. The Zonave jacket is a glittering mass of. heavy gold embroidery, and a long veil of clear gauze is fastened on with an Oriential gold ornament. Robin Hood is dressed in green cloth, and he should carry a bow and quiver. A little great • grandmother in a huge bonnet, mittens, and carrying a bag makes a good get-up. Queen Christmas may wear any cheerful. looking dress. This should be trimmed with holly, &Q.' and a band bearing the words "A Merry Christmas," across the breast. Another boy's costume is that of a clown, and this is always popular. A miniature policeman should be dressed of course, in blue, with belt and helmet, and he should carry a lantern and truncheon.

I am afraid moat brides-elect have rather magni- fied ideas about the number of dresses necessary for their trousseau. Unless one is going out a good deal, a dinner dress, a %mart afternoon cos- ta/Me (gointaway gown), a good, serviceable taller-made, and a warm and comfortable tea- gown, whioh eon be worn at breakfast; are as many as need be had. Should you go to a dance, it is far better to buy a dress fresh for the occasion. But you could include a very smart and dainty ball bodice in your trousseau, such as you could weer wtth any skirt. And you will want a silk biome, and a good winter coat, up-to-date in every particular. A sewing-machine is such a help to the home dressmaker and economist.

CHAPTER XI 111. IN PIIRSU: What say you now? What umfort have we now ? RICHARD IL Lord Grosvenor arrived z 6 Dalton a little later than he had expected. ano proceeded directly to the inn to freshen his toilet before seeking his young betrothed. The first person he encountered on entering the common parlour was Lord Adlowe. There was a mocking light in Adlowe's eyes, and a mocking smile on his lips as he bowed to his young rival that chilled Paul Grosvenor, with a sudden apprehension. Concealing this as best he could, the young lord acknowledged the salutation gravely, and would have passed on, but that Adlowe stretched out his hand and detained him. "We are well met, Lord Grosvenor," said Ad- lowe, with a great display of politeness. "It seems we are here on the same errand. Truly women are powerful, since one delicate girl has drawn you from the charminz seclusion of the Eagle's Eyrie, and me from the fascinations of society. I have just discovered that Miss Are- valo—or shall I call her Miss Trevelyau ?—is here." "Permit me to pass, Lord Adlowe," said young Grosvenor, haughtily. "I decline to hear the lady's name from your lips." An evil glance shot from Adlowe's eyes, and he stepped aside. "You carry a high hand," he said, "because you think you will win. We shall see! We shall Bee ! " There was a world of hidden meaning in his words and looks and tones, but Grosvenor dis- dained to question him, or prolong the conversa- tion_ He c.q.s-zie'out into the hall, and remained there until' lhe landlord came up to announce that his room was ready, and to conduct him to

Strange Freaks of Street Ruffians.

The Paris street ruffians amused themselves last win- ter by squirting vitriol on the dresses of ladies, for the simple pleasure they derived from uselesshestruction. Many of them were arrested and were condemned to prison. It was imagined the pehalties had been severe enough to cure the stupid young men of their senseless pastime, hut it seems that is net the case, for, thoutili they have this winter refrained from squirting vitriol, they have commenced using scissors. Within the last few days Police Commissaries of the Boulevard quarters have received visits from very numerous fashionably dressed ladies, who came to complain of large pieces br- ing cut out of their dresses and mantles while they were walking in the streets. On Monday two ladies, accom- panied by their husbands, were at the Salle des De- peches of the "Figaro." when one of them felt a pull at , her mantle. Turning round,she saw that an individual, who sought to make good his escape, had cut a big hole in it, and she noticed that her friend's dress was ruined by a large piece having been cut out of it. The twe husbands rushed upon the man, who was captured, and taken to the Police Commissary's office,where his pocket was found to contain no fewer than nine large pieces of silk and velvet he had cut out of ladies' dresses. He confessed his stupid offence, and was sent to Old Police Depot.

"If you will give me your name, sir, I will register it," said the landlord, throwing open the door of his second-best room, Adlowe having taken the best. "It is Grosvenor," replied our hero. "Not the young Lord Grosvenor of the Eagle's Eyrie ? " cried the landlord, between hope and fear. "The same." The landlord was in a transport of delight that his hotel should be so prospered as to be patronised by two noble lords at once. "I have heard of you, my lord," he said, with his most obsequious bow. "The Eyrie is one of the noblest monuments of our county, and we feel what I may call, not knowi ig how else to express it, a national pride in it I The Grosvenors are an English race, but they have a seat in Wales, and we Welshmen feel a sort of property in them. You will find congenial society at the Harp just now, I don't doubt, my lord. Lord Adlowe is stopping here." "Yes, I met him in the parlour," said Grosvenor, • carelessly. "How long as he been here 7" "Since yesterday morning, my lord.', Grosvenor started. "So long as that ?" he said, involuntarily. "Yes, my lord. He is touring, I think. Last night he went on a little excursion to the north- ward, taking his valet with him. He came back this afternoon alone, and the horses seemed to have been hard driven, but his lordship paid munificently for their use. We have fine horses, my lord, if you want to make excursions. This is not the best season, however. Lord Adlowe had a heavy mist to travel in." This information was extremely valuable to Lord Grosvenor, who began to speculate in his own mind, as to why Adlowe should have made a night excur- sion in the rain, when he must already have become informed of Giralda's retreat. "He intends to carry her off," he thought, "and he has been looking up a place to take her to. I think my arrival is most opportune." He dismissed the landlord, brushed his hair and his garments, and set out without delay to seek Giralda. A street urchin, encouraged by the gift of a bright silver sixpence, conducted him to Mrs. Jones's pastry-cook shop. Mrs. Jones herself was seated behind the little counter, her eyes red with weeping. She was alone, and presented such a forlorn picture that Grosvenor instinctively felt that something sinister had happened. "You are Mrs. Jones ? " he inquired. The woman replied in the affirmative, eying him suspiciously. "I am Lord Grosvenor," communicated our hero, his bright, frank, handsome face, with its debonair and innocent expression, dissipating her suspicions and arousing her interest at once. "I am the friend of your lodger, Miss Arevalo and am come to see her iu answer to a letter which I received from her last night." Mrs. Jones fell to sobbing boisterously. Grosvenor looked at her in astonishment, begin- ning to apprehend that some misfortune had oe- cured to Giralda. "Take ins up to her !" he exclaimed. "Oh, I wish to Heaven I could ! " sobbed Mrs. Jones. "The poor pretty creetur ! And that dear, boy, too ! This is a sorry day for me, my lord. "What is the mattet? " demanded Grosvenor. "Where is Miss Arevalo ? "I don't know ! I don't know!" "Not know ? Is she not here ?" "No, my lord. She went out last night, and it a-raining in a mist-like; and she took the little boy with her, which he wanted to go for to protect her, and I haven't seen either of them since." Grosvenor was stunned at this intelligence. "And there was another, my lord, in here, in- quiring for her about noon," continued Mrs. Jones, still sobbing, "and he was that mad because she was gone that I expected the roof to come off. His name was Lord A Ilowe." "lie was here' to-day, then ?" asked Grosvenor, arousing himself from his stupor. "Yes, my lord, but I couldn't tell him anything. There was a woman here, too—her old nurse—that book on fearful. I have searched the whole town. but no one saw Miss Giralda after she left my door." "At what time did she go out ?" "It was between seven and eight, my lord, as near as I can tell." "Where did she set out to go?" "To the doctor's, my loud, to carry a tumbler of 'ally and some cakes that she wanted to send to a tick man." Grosvenor looked puzzled and bewildered. "The sick man was a friend of hers," explained inn. Jones. "He got hurt on the Burnt Downs, and was taken to the cottage of my friend, Peggy Willsey. I should think that Miss Giralda might have gone to Peggy's, only that her umbrella and jelly and cakes were all found on the street corner this morning, broken and wet and spoilt —leastways the jelly and cakes were." " That looks as if she had been forcibly cap- tured and carried off," mused Grosvenor, his heart a prey to the keenest apprehensions. "And this is all you know, Mrs. Jones ?" Mrs. Jones replied that it was, and offered to send a guide with his lordship, if he should choose to visit the doctor and pursue his inquiries. Gros- venor answered by acceptinv her offer, and the good woman called in the urchin who had served him before as guide, and who was still lingering about the door. Grosvenor hastened to the doctor's residence, had the good fortune to find the physician at hoine, and stated at once who he was, and what he wanted. "The young lady was not here last night," de- clared the physician. "She said she should call every night to inquire aiter my new patient, but I did not expect her last night on account of the rain. If she had come, I had good news for her. I have broken up the fever that seriously threat- ened, although I assured the young lady it must run its course. The man is weak, and he is in a low state, but I see no reason why he shouldn't get well." "Who is this man in whom Miss Arevalo took BO marked an interest ? " asked Grosvenor. "There is quite a little romance connected with him," said the doctor. "He is, it appears a lover of Peggy Willsey's who has been missing these eighteen years. He has been in Australia. and so fled from the town ? " he asked himself. "No ; she had been taken away by violence, and Adlowe was the man who took her. His last night's excursion was to remove her to some re- treat, and Wigby remains with her as her jailer. I will see Adlowe, and force the truth from him." He entered the inn, sought the landlord, and demanded to see Lord Adlowe.

"His lordship went away directly after you went out, my lord," replied the innkeeper. "He will not come back again." Grosvenor's face grew stern at this confirmation of his worst fears. . .

"How and where did he go ? " he inquired. "He went on his own horse, and to the north- ward. His lordship did not say where." "I suppose he went to the same place he visited last night," said Grosvenor' thoughtfully, having no faith that Adlowe had been "northward" at all. The landlord hesitated and smiled.

"This is market-day," he observed, "and one or two farmers says to me this noon that they see my black pair, all sweat, coming from Pencoed way this morning. They must have been mis- taken ; but whether they were or not, I got well paid ! " Grosvenor's mind was instantly made up. This remark of the farmer's was but the simplest straw, but "straws,' as the old adage says, "show which way the wind blows." He determined to go to Pencoed. _ _ _

To think, with him, was to act. He paid his bill, went down to the station and took the train for Cardiff. He changed there for Pencoed. The train on which he found himself on the shore line was an exprees. The coach into which he entered had already two occunants. He started when he recognised them as the Lady Beatrice Hampton and her attendant, Mary Fleck. Her ladyship put out her hand to him in smiling welcome, and motioned him to a seat beside her. "I am on my way to Hamptonwold," she said. "We get out at Pencoed, where the carriage will be waiting for us. When did you leave the Eyrie ?" "This morning," replied Grosvenor. "My guests arrived last night safely. They are now at the Eyrie, and 1 think the precautions taken have effectually prevented pursuit." The Lady Beatrice smiled comprehendingly, and breathed a sigh of relief. "I have been very anxious," she murmured. 1I have had many fears, but now, I trust, all will be well. I have stolen away from town and from my father for a week's rest near my dear ones. Lord Trevelyan is engaged in a wild search for the Countess Arevalo ' and Giralda, and at one moment he sighs and the next threatens. I believe he intends returning home to-morrow." "I have been to Dalton to-day, Lady Beatrice," said Grosvenor.

"You have? You have seen my darlings, then? Is Meggy with them ? " " No ; she was there thi3 morning, but left be- fore noon. Can you bear more ill news, dear Lady Beatrice? Adlowe has again obtained possession of Giralda and Fay !" • It seemed as if the tortured heart of Lady Bea- trice could bear little more. She uttered a low cry of utter despair, which was lost in the shrill whistle of the engine as the train moved out of the station.

"Tell me all," she whispered, grasping his hand. "Let me know the worst at once !" Lord Grosvenor hastened to narate to her all that he had himself learned from various sources, adding: "I feel a conviction that Giralda and Fay have been taken to, or near, Pencoed. I feel equally sure that I shall find her. Trust in me, dear Lady Beatrice, and believe that you will soon clasp your children in your arms !" The Lady Beatrice, under his hopeful encourage- ment, felt her own hopes re\ ive. "If you do find them, Paul,' she said earnestly, "You shall marry Giralda at an early day. I can- not guard her, situated as I am, and as long as she is unmarried Adlowe will pursue her !" Grosvenor expressed his joy and gratitude at this assurance of a speedy marriage, but he had not needed it to quicken the ardour of his pursuit or the keenness of his search. _ _ _

"If you rescue them, lodge them safely in Pen- coed," said the Lady Beatrice, "and let me know at once." Grosvenor promised. The journey to Penceed was speedily accomplished, and the travellers alighted. The Hampton carriage was in waiting, and good Meggy Fleck, her vail down, stood near in waiting for her mistress and sister. Leaving the Lady Beatrice to hear Megg's story, Lord Grosvenor strolled up the street towards the inn. "Perhaps I was foolish," he thought, "to heed the simple and easual mention of this place by a farmer. Gira.lda's prison may be halfway to Dalton, or in Entirely another direction. And perhaps," he added, " the mention of Pencoed was a clue thrown clirectly into my hands by a kind Provi- dence. I shall soon know." He continued his walk, proceeding slowly. The sound of voices in altercation within a dingy little ale-house, as he was passing, called his attention, and he casually glanced in. There were three or four men standing at the bar drinking. One of these, a stout, red-faced man, already more than half intoxicated, Lord Grosvenor recognised. The man was Bittly, of the bluff-cottage! In an instant Grosvenor comprehended that he had been guided aright—that he sas near Giralda's 'orison.

Some Remedies for Headaches.

Dr. Andrew Wilson, writing on this subject in " Health," gives the following remedies, which will be useful to your irquirir.g correspondent, also to your many readers, headache b-lug an everyday occurrence : —Headache from brain nerres%ion, 1 teaspoonful of Bishop's granular hydro-hromate of caffeine in a half glass of water, taken while effervescing. Ammornia chloride, 3 drachms ; acetate morphia, 1 grain ; citrate caffeine, 30 grains ; aromatic spirits of ammonia, 1 drachm ;e lixir of guarana, 4 oz,; rose. water, 4 oz ; Mix. • A dessert spoonful to he taken at intervals of 10 or 15 minutes. Bromide of potash, 2 3, or 5 grain grain tabloids in half glass of water when the lace is turgid. Phosphorus, 30 drops of phosphoric acid (dilute) in halt tumbler of water, thrice daily after meals. Lauder Brunton, to be serviceable, either in the form of a full dose, to secure bri,k purgation, or small doses, taken twice daily, when frontal headache is associated with consti- pation. He also recommends 10 drops of nitro-muriatic (dilute) in a wine glassful of water, when the bowels are already acting normally. Soda bicarbonate, 10 grains in water before meals. This is most serviceable when fron- tal headache is high up, at the margin of the hairy scalp ; the nitro-muriatic acid being preferable to it when the headache is located at the eyebrows. Oil of peppermint.—The Chinese oil may be used by wetting a needle with it, and drawing the point slowly across the forehead; a sense of coolness along the track of the needle follows, and in many cases the headache quickly disappears. Hot water, applied by means of a towel or flannel to the head. In using this remedy, it must be rernenibered that the water should be as hot as can be well borne. CHAPTER XLIX.

LORD GRoSVENOR TO THE •RESCUE. My heart is firm: There's uaugnt within the compass of humanity But I would dare and do. SIR A. HUNT. On beholding Bittly in the ale-house, Lord Gros- venor'e resolution was taken at once. He would watch and follow him. He felt a conviction which no reasoning could either strengthen or weaken, that Bittly had coins to Pencoed to be of service to Lord Adlowe, and that by following him he should arrive at Giralda's prison. He slouched his hat over his face, pulled up his coat collar, and walked up and clown the street, keeping a vigilant eye upon the ale-house door. Occasionally he went close to it and looked in. During the progress of his self-imposed vigil the evening came on, and the lamps were lighted in the shops. The keeper of the ale-house caine out and lighted a great red lantern over his door, and lights gleamed from his windows, and sounds of merri- ment issued from his establishment. Growing uneasy and anxious, Lord Grosvenor at length entered the ale-shop, and called for a glass of beer. While it was being drawn he employed his eyes to good purpose. The shop Was an ordinary specimen of ite class. The floor was covered wtth saw-dust ; the counter laden with bottles and an array of tumblers. On the side oh the room opposite the bar were several sniall tables, and about these men were congre- gated with drink mild pipes. Through the curl;ng smoke Lord Grosvenor dis- cerned Bittly, now quite iutoxieated and helpless, his head drooping ou his breast, his figure leaning heavily against his nearest neignhour, who was in little better condition. The confined and tainted air of the place nearly made Lord Grosvenor ill. He paid for his ale and made a feint of sipping it, still keeping his gaze upon the nearly unconscious Bittly. The ale-house keeper's glances followed his. "A little overcome, sir," he said, with a smile. "He doesn't live in town, sir. I shall have to make him up a bed for to-uight. He seems to have plenty of money." As he concluded, the landlord summoned an assistant, and gave orders that Bittly should be housed for the night. Grosvenor set down his glass and went out into the fresh air of the street. "I cannot follow him to-night," he thought, dis- appointedly. "I could not question the landlord, lest he tell Bittly in the morning, and so put Giralda's jailers on their guard. I must wait till morning." With a sigh he took his way to the inn. At daybreak he was astir again, and as soon as the shops began to open he was back. at the ale- house. He found the landlord behind the bar, arranging bottles, but the room had no other occupant. Calling for a glass of ale, he carelessly inquired after the inebriate of the previous night. "Oh, that man 1" said the landlord, sociably, "he's a stranger here, and knows enough to keep a close tongue between his teeth. •He likes a glass pretty well. I don't know where he lives, but he said he should be back again to-night. He has taken a fancy to some of our jolly fellows." "I should think," remarked Grosvenor, "that he would hardly get away before night, judging from the condition he was in last evening " Oh, he was off before daylight, sir, returned tht; " hfs wife, I shouldn't werdiers,P:

rosvenor concealed his feeling of disappoint- rnslnt, and went back to the inn to make inquiries there concerning any house newly let, or any strangers who might have recently come to the

place: His inquiries proved to be vain and useless, no one being. able to give him any information. Nothing remained, therefore, but to wait for Bittly's next visit to the ale-liouse, and to keep a more vigi- lant watch upon his movements.

The day being on his hands with nothing to do, Lord Grosvenor rode down to Ilamptonwold, spend- ing several hours with the Lady Beatrice, whom he found dispirited and gloomy. She informed him that a painful sense of bereavement haunted her in every moment, sleeping or waking, and that, un- able to bear it longer, she had written to her hus- band to pay her a secret visit, bringing Rupert with him.

"I may be indiscreet," she said, with a passion- ate tremor of her voice that went to Grosvenor's heart, "but I am nervous, and troubled, and care- worn. I have told Geoffrey to meet me in the little Persian summer-house to-morrow morning at ten. He will be there. Oh, that we might die there together, and end all this vain and terrible struggle 1" "Say not so, dear Lady Beatrice!" exclaimed Grosvenor, smiling. "The meeting will be a little secret romance, without danger or peril. I will bring Giralda and Fay to share in it." The Lady Beatrice looked at him, her gloomy eyes transfigured with a sudden and glorious light. She had been mourning for her lost children, and had become unusually despondent. Grosvenor's promise made her actually radiant. "Bring them to me ! " she exclaimed, "and all this burden of care will drop from me. I want to look in Giralda.'s eyes and see that she is still bright and hopeful. I want to clasp my boy—my youngest boy—to my bosom, and let him nestle there as he used to. Then my courage and hope fulness will come back, and I can feel strong to do and bear again 1 " "They shall be here," declared Grosvenor, solemnly. "We will have a little family reunion to-morrow morning. I will bring Giralda and Fay here unseen."

"We cannot be too careful," rejoined the Lady BetArice, her face again clouding. "After I had dispatched my letter, to the Eyrie, I received a note from my father that he and Lord Trevelyan would be here to-day, and that the marquis was in a very disconsolate mood, and had been induced to visit Hamptonwold a day or two. Ido not want to be rash, but I have trodden on the brink of dangers so long that I have grown careless of them.'

Lord Grosvenor encouraged her ladyship with his bright hopefulness, and when he left her in the afternoon she was in a much brighter mood. It was sunset when he re-entered Pencoed.

It was twilight when he again took up his station in front of the ale-house. __ _ _ _

Bittly was not there. . The young lord knew well that Mrs. Bittly was a virago, and that her vixenish qualities were always greatly excited by her husband's intemperate habits and he feared that his man would not make his appearance that night. In this he was mistaken. He was working him- self up into a desperate mood, when Bittly came swaggering down the street, and strode into the ale-house with the air of one who feels himself a monarch.

"He has been taking something already," thought Grosvenor. "Alter being out so long last night I believe he'll go home early to-night. He wouldn't dare repeat his offence so soon." Events proved that he was right. An hour or two passed, during which the mer- riment waxed high in the ale-house, and the young lord paced to and fro impatiently, first on one side of the street, then on the other, his tall, slight figure closely mnffied, and his hat drawn down over his noble, high-bred, anxious face. And then came his reward.

Bittly, swaggering, and talking loudly, came out with a companion, and the two reeled up the street

arm in arm.

Lord Grosvenor, his heart beating high, followed close behind. them. _ _ _ _

The men paused near the outskirts of the place and had a short colloquy, after which they parted ; with a burst of maudlin sentiment, Grosvenor con- waling himself in.the shadow of a gate-way. Then Bittly struck out on one of the country roads, reeling and singing snatches of bacchanalian songs, Grosvemz keeping near to him. In this way nearly three miles were traversed. They had come within sight of the house where Giralda and Fay were confined, and Lord Grosvenor was regarding it earnestly and curiously, some in- stinct telling him that he was near Giralda's prison, when Bittly suddenly deposited himself on the round in the shadow of a hedge, and fell to weep- ing and moaning in a maudlin manner. Grosvenor came up to him. "What is the matter, my man ?" he demanded. Bittly was too much intoxicated to recognize his Lordship, whose features were still half-concealed, or to feel any su, prise at his salutation. " noth'n !" he returned, tearfully. g‘ That is, 'tan't none o' yonr business, as nobody knows on. Le'me 'lone! G'way !" "But if I could help you," suggested Grosvenor, jingling a handful of silver. Bittly raised himself on his elbow with a momen- tary display of eagerness, and then he sank back, grumbling: " Don't want no money, no nothing I Wish was back •t Little Grosvenor, hanged 'f dont ! 01' woman scold, scold, scold! Wish was dead and done with 't !" and he snivelled. Lord Grosvenor saw that the dread of impending evil was strong with the man. Where do you live ?" he asked. Bittly cocked his eye knowingly, gave his hat a push on one side, and looked suspiciously at his questioner. The next moment the liquor he had taken overcame his caution, and he answered, with another burst of tears: • "Over there !" indicating with an unsteady fore- 4nger the house Lord Grosvenor had noticed. "Dog's life! Go out jolly, have good time, catch it when get home! Wish was laid in my narrer grave, with a weeping wilier dropping leaves on to me. Life an't no object!' Grosvenor thought a moment. He was deter- mined to effect Giralda's rescue that very night; but how? He considered the idea of going back to Pen- coed, and procuring assistance and effecting the rescue by force, but he discarded it speedily. Such a course would only provoke a gossip and a scandal, which to say the least would be highly injudicious, the more especially as the Earl of Hampton anti the Marquis of Trevelyan had al- ready arrived in the neighbourhood, and were now at Haniptonwold. He thought of waiting till morning, and going to the house disguised as a pedlar, but clearly he would gain nothing by that. No pedlar would, of course, gain access to the captives. No; it was plain that he must move quickly, and on this very night. In a brief space he had thought out a• course of action. Bittly had sunk down into a sort of drowsiness, quite oblivious of his interlocutor. Lord Gros- venor touched him with his foot, partially arousing him. ' "See here, my man !" he exclaimed. "I want to make a temporary exchange with you. Lend me your coat and hat and muffler, and you shall have my top-coat, which is a fine, thick one, ad a sovereign besides." "Makes no difference," replied Bittly, drowsily. "Where's the money?" Interpreting this as a consent, Grosvenor re- lieved the man of the garments he had mentioned, substituting his own hat and top-coat upon the limp and lifeless figure, Bittly all the while whin- ing and protesting. "Can't you le'rne 'lone?" he snivelled. "Wakin' honest man up this hour the night? Have the law on you. Never had no peaae my life since day was born. Always trouble. Always knocked about like mislle old tenpin. G'way !" The exchange of garments being effected, Lord Grosvenor permitted the man to relapse into drowsiness while he put on over his own the gar- ments he had borrowed. Bittly was about as tall as himself, but stouter. Having on two coats, however, Lord Grosvenor exhibited very near the amplitude required to eilect a resemblance. The old ragged woollen muffler tied twice about his neck and over the chin, as Bittly had worn it, end the old, worn, slouched hat, with its torn brim' pulled over his face, made him look startlingly like the man to Whom they beLonged. not ebiliv, beim,- on the contra,-.:. quite warm for April. Yet Lord Grosvenor covered I the recumbent, sleeping man carefully, laid his own hat closer over his face to screen it from the dews, and then gloved at a quick pace toward the house.

-As he went along he put his hand in Bittly's coat- pocket, and discovered a home-key. "So far, good ! " he mentally ejaculated, his spirits rising. "I can enter the house at will. The rest I shall leave to the emergency." As he came nearer to the dwelling he eyed 1111-ninto cloaely marking tha boaasil uwer

'That is her room," he thought, his face flush- ing with eagerness. "My darling ! She does not dream that I am so near." _

He stood at the gate a moment, his eyes upraised. A faint gleam of light above the boarding of the window told him that the occupant of the room had not yet retired. He was tempted to tall to Giralda, and make known to her his presence, but he re- strained the wild impulse, and walked slowly: and cautiously around the house, to take a preliminary view of the premises. The only light on the lower floor came from the kitchen --- a room much more congenial to Mrs. Bittly than the parlour. Lord Grosvenor halted at the window, and looked in through an opening in the shutter. M rs. Bittly and Wigby were within. A discon- tented scowl disfigured the hard visage of the foimer. The latter was sipping, quite at his eases a bowl of pinch which he had just brewed. "Of course," he was saying, his tones readily penetrating to Grosvenor's ears, "after being up all last night, and on guard, too, I shall go to bed t,-night. I am sleepy, and am not fit to keep watch, and what is more, 1 don't intend to do it."

Mrs. Bittly scowled still more fiercely. "1 never could stand it to sit up," she muttered. "I've been on my feet all day, and am just tired out. Bittly ought to keep watch, and to behave himself. I wish I had him here this minute." _

"I wrote to my lord this morning, to the ad- dress he gave," said Wigby, tranquilly, "telling him of Bittly's drunkenness of last night, IBS lordship will be along in the morning, I know. He would never trust a drunken nor a drinking man with his secrets. How do we know that Ba- tty ain't letting out the whole story in some ale- house ?"

Mrs. Bittly assented, again expressing a desire to lay hands on her truant husband. "I ought to have left him at home," she acknow- ledged. "If my lord comes in the morning, I shall ask to send him home."

By this time Wigby had finished his punch. Se now arose, took up his candle, and declared that he was tired, and should leave the care of the house to her.

Mrs. Bittly grumbled a little, and stated her be- lief that all would be safe, since the captives were- securely locked in their room, and expressed her determination also to retire.

" Nobody 'll get traa of the girl here," she added, taking up the remaining candle. "And if they do, they won't get the key of her room, which I keeps it in my own pocket, she added, deter- minedly. "Let 'em come 1" So saying, she withdrew, and Wigby also de- parted to his own room, which happoned to be situated in the uppermost story, and at some dis- tance from that of his assistants.

"CA.PITAI,"

The Liberal candidate for the Evesham Division Will find himself strangely out of gear with his old political associations. His pamphlet " Three Acres and a Cow "had a preface by Mr. Chamberlain and an appendix by the Duke of Argyll. It seems un- necessary to add that the views of which it was the advocate were appropriated by .N 1 r. Jesse Collings. This was perhaps on the principle that brothers who are of a size may blamelessly use each other's shirts, trousers, and overcoats, for Mr. Impev may be said to be of the Chamberlain household, having a busi- ness in Birmingham, and being a Warwickshire man by birth. •

The negotiations for the release of the dynamit- ards continue. There is no doubt that we shall have definite news on the subject oon. Ido not bear that Sir William Harcourt has been wen over yet, but it is not improbable that he may be. The fact is that Daly is sure to be released sooner or lat- er. None of the others will he set at liberty. But in Daly's case there are special reasons which would .enable even a Tory Government to give way. All through his ease there was a suggeslon of police Provocation which serves as a legitimate reason for giving him a relaxation of his sentence.

Mr. Impey is one of the founders of V-- Old-age Pension League, the establishment of .ch is the ?float practical step yet taken into everday work- th.g machinery the suggstieTls of Mr. Charles Booth, Sir Walter Foster, and Dr. Hunter. I hear 'that one of the features of the Evesham elec- tion is not unlikely to be the production of a Working scheme, which will probably be introduced at the beginning of next session by private mem- _hers on the Liberal side, in conjunction with the Government. This, in fact, in view of the alleged obstruction of Mr. Chamberlain to the prompt con- elusion and reporting of the Commission, is the only Way of checkmaking such tactics. If private mem- bers can secure a day, there is no reason why Dr. Hunter's scheme, modified an amplified, should not be carried next. session, to the discomfiture of Mr. Chamberlain's obstructive strategy. On these 'and other grounds, I am convinced, from what I hear, that the Evesham election is going to be fought in the most spirited way by the Liberal Party, that the weapons brought into play will be many and effective, and that every strategic move of the enemy will be promptly outflanked. I hear that all the most active and best known of the county Radicals who won the Parish Councils Act and were the authors of its best provisions will go down and give much more time to this than to Previous elections.

It was not in Mr, Gladstone's nature to avoid availing himself of the opportunity afforded by the visit of the Armenian deputation to Hawarden to express the indignation that has been seething in bis brain respecting Turk6h misgovernment and -oppression. My anticipation that he could not long abstain from launching in public some of the burn- ing invectives he has been fulminating in private has been realised even more promptly than I anti- cipated. The strength of his denunciation of the Government that is "a disgrace to civilisation and a curse to "unkind" is in no way surprising to those who, seeing how completely the story of the Cruelties has moved him, were perfectly sure they Would burst the bonds of the walls at Hawarden, and that the voice never silent on behalf of humanity and truth would make itself heard to the World. and reverberate in the ears of the anything but Sublime Porte.

The britiv arrived at the hotel where the ceremony took place in a vehicle drawn by a team of twelve oxen, and was elegantly dressed in ecru sateen, trimmed with b.ight blue silk, and Wore a red hat. Her father, M!lkAa Mkiza, who gave her away, ware, as did the bridegroom, the conventional tall hat and frock coat. The wedding presents are described, in the customary phraseo- lody, as numerous and coitly, and the ceremony Was emphasised by the discharge of firearms and the throwing of old boots, rice, -&c., at the newly united couple.

The charges which the Armenian Association bring against the Foreign Office are applicable to The Foreign Ministers of both parties, and as events - in Armenia have approached their present culmina- tion, they are more applicable to the present Government than to their predecessors. But the association should complete its Armenian pro- Rramme. Suppose that the Porte refuses point- blank to make the political and administrative changes in Armenia which the association calls for, what then? Is•he to be compelled, and if so how? Does the association imagine Oat a European concert on the subject is in the least degree Possible? Will Germany stir a single Pomeranian,? Will France launch one solitary soldier? Will Russia send a single Cossack into Armenia, and thus enter into war with Turkey? Is England to force her ships through the Bosphorus and threaten lc bombard Constantinople? The association must Itcow that not one of these things can be done,that the attempt to dá them would set Europe in ablaze, and that so long as the Porte refuses to act, any attempt to compel her would he utterly futile Unless Russia were commissioned to take posses- sion of the province. It is going too far for the responsible functionaries of the association to accuse Lord Kimberley and Sir E. Grey of discredit- able and dishonourable acts, and to assert that the Present Foreign Office is a disgrace to England. 'The association must be definite, and say whether Europe is to declare war against Turkey, if diplo- toatie pre:sure fails to procure from the Porte the I very long order of the rts,,oeiation.

It is difficult to see why the Tories should be- grudge the Liberals the rare satisfaction of wel- coming within their ranks the repentant Conser- vative peer. It may be safely claimed for the Liberals that they may have made no great fuss over the Earl of Buckinghamshire's declaration that he has emerged from the gloom am negation of Conservatism into the bright hepetulness of the Liberal creed. Yet surely the event is rare enough to justify some little jubilation, and even Toryism, one would think, may well spare one of its redun- dant peers for the sake of demonstrating to in- credulous Liberalism that at least good things may come off the Conservative benches. But then it is not every peer who has the blood of John Hampden in his veins. His lordship's motto—" There is no going back "—is significant, as indicative of the ir- reversible character of his decision.

THE FINAL MEETING

The Runcorn Board of Improvement Commissioners held their last meeting on Friday. Mr. S. Taylor, J. P, occupied the chair, and there were also present. Messrs. E. Imison, W. Collier, J. W. Lightburn, J Pritchard, D. Bisbrown, J. Meadoweroft, W. Flankinson, F. A. Lake (law clerk) and E. Marshall (secretary and account- ant.) TUE COST OF 'FHB RECENT ELECTIONS. The principal or heaviest damage is to the machinery plant, situated on the ground for, and which was of a most expensive kind. The two storeys ahnee were used by workmen, and contained many valuable chests of tools, as well as a large quantity of furniture, some of which was about to be sent to cus- tomers. In the rooms were extensive stocks of fancy woods of great value. Those have also been entirely destroyed. The damage is estimated to be about £3,000, but fortunately the premises were insured. About forty men will be thrown out of employment, as well as other folks not engaged at the works. It is expected that a temporary workshop will be obtained in order that work may be resumed.

MEETINGS OF PARISH COUNCILS.

HALTON.

In the Girls' Schoolroom, Halton. on Monday evening, the first parish meeting was held. The chair at the outset of the meeting was occupied by Mr. Gamon. There were also present the Rev. G. D Wrar. Messrs. J. Stokes, James Looker, R. Hughes, J. S. Jsaeock, H. Reynolds, John AckPrlev. Wm. McCullock, Jeseph At- bury, Simnel Barrow, Charles Birch. James Randal Land Woodier (assistant overseer).—The Chairman hoped the Council would have pleasant meetings, and that the members would work harmoniously together through- out their term of office. He hoped they would sink all differences and work together for t e benefit of the parish.—Mr. McCullock then proposed that Mr. Stokes he chairman of the Council, and in doing so spoke of the fitness of the gentleman for the position. Hereferred to the fact of the electors haying placed h'm at the head of the poll.—Mr. Hughes seconded—Mr. Randall propneed I the They G. D. Wray for the position of chairman. He said Mr. Stokes lived too far out of the parish. When- ever they wanted to see the chairman on the business of the Council they did not want to go to R( ck Savage.— Mr. Birch seconded.—Mr. Stokes sail he had no desire for the chairmanship. It had been a matte" of great pain to him to see religion introdeeed into ne affairs of the parish. If this was introduced into the meetings he would he the very first tn put his foot down against it, —Mr. Barrow did not see that the distance of the Chair- man's residence need to come up at a meeting like that. The clerk would answer any wiestions.—Mr. Looker sail the present Act was to make things different from what they were. If they elected the vicar to the chair they would be in the same position as before as re- garded the chairman. —Mr. Jeacoek said the nrinelpai duties of the Parish Council would he'the Burial Blard and the charities. No one knew so moeh about these matters as the Vicar, and he should support the appointment of Mr. Wray as the chairman.— Mr. Finely s said he would like to ask the Vicar whether he sent letters to the Church candidates, stating that it was not his wish to be chairman, hut that it was the wish of the Church party that Mr. Randall should ho the chairman.—Several members denied this.—The Rev. G. D. Wray asked the chairman whether the question was in order, to which he replied that it was not—Mr. Bar- low asked whether it was not only a provisional chair- man that was to be elected until March.—The Rev. G. D. Wray said that was so, owing to a mistake in the Act.—Mr. Looker nominated Mr. Gamon for the posi- tion of chairman until March.—Mr. Barlow seconded.— After a long discussion a vote was taken, when it was found that seven voted for the Rev. G. D. Wray and four for Mr. Gamon. Mr. Hughes did not vote.—The Rev. G D Wray, in taking the position, said he had always taken it previously by virtne of his office, but by boina elected hat the Council his position was far stronger. —Mr Reynolds proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Gamon fe.r ths admirable manner in which h° had performed his duties as chairman at the previous Parish Meetings. —Mr. Barron seconded, and Mr. Stokes, the Rev. G P. Wray and Mr. Looker supported, and it was carried unanimonsly.—Mr. Gamon responded to the vote.—The nonintment of clerk was then proceeded with, ard Mr. Woodier was selected to the post. The salary to he paid was to he determined at a future date.—Mr. Halland,of Parr's Banking Co.,was appointed treasurer to the Coun- cil.—Tt was decided to defer the financial business until the next meeting. FIRE AT WARRINGTON.

On New Year" Eve, a most disastrous fire occurred in the cabinet woks of Messrs. S. E. and R. Johnson, Ship Yard, off Bridte•st. The fire was first observed by Mr. Stiorrock,, of 9, and 11, Ship Yard, who saw smoke issuing from the top stnrey of the building. He reported the matter to P.C.'s Amson and Marsh, and the latter at once ran to the fire bell to give the alarm. Superintendent Turner, with three men and the hose cart, at once proceeded to the show rooms situated partially in Bridge-st., and partially in Ryland s-st. They were subsequent!, told that the fire was in the cabinet works in Ship Yard, and proceeded thither. Shortly after the departure of the Superintendent and the reel cart, the Deputy-SuperintenCent followed with the steam engine Major, the latter being under the charge of Engineer Ratcliffe. When the works were reached by the brigade the fire had obtained a firm bold. Two powerful branches were got to work from the engine, one for each storey of the burning premises. Another branch was attached to the large main in Bridge-street, and was employed in preventing the tire obtaining a hold of the cottages in Lower Ship-yard, the occupants of which had become so alarmed as to remove their household furniture into the attest, and from thence into the houses of their neighbours. A hose pipe was also continually kept playing upon the rear of the wozd turning premises of the late Mr. John Cress, Rose and Crown-street. where a large quantity of valuable wood viaB stored. A WARRINGTON "DEATH. TRAP."

SEVERE SIRICTURES BY THE CORONER.

Mr. Brighouse held an inquest at the Police Station, n Monday, on the body ok Ellen Ashbrook, aged 13 oyears, who was drowned in what is known as " Bclton's pit," off Orford-lane, on the previ- ons Saturday. Mr. J. Lyon Whitile (town clerk) was present on behalf of the Warrington Corporation, and Mr. Stanley Fair also represented Messrs. Bolton and Sons.

Joseph Ashbrook, father of the deceased, said that he resided at 56. Forshaw-st. He last saw his daughter alive at Ireakla4 time on Saturday morning when he left for work. At dinner time she was out, but he thought that she had probably gone to see one of her aunts. Towards three o'clock he sent to one of her aunts far her, but she was not there. It was blowing very strong at the time. He did not think it likely that she would take away her life, as nothing had occurred which would give her cause t 4 do so. She was very jolly at breakfast time. Her body was brought home on Sunday.

Elizabeth Ashbrook, sister of the deceased, said that at eleven o'clock she went to her sister, who was play- ing with her brothers in the yard, instead of doing the household work, and told her that she would tell her father if she did not do her work. She replied, "Get it done yourself." Her father never beat her, nor did witness intend to tell her father about her, in fact, she did not mention the affair until it was discovered that her si.ter wag missing. Her sister frequentiv went to her aunt's houqe in Winwick- road, and had to cross the piece of land near the pit where she was found. The fence round the pit was down in some parts. _ _ _ _ _ . .

Emily Ashcroft said that she met her sister after she had left home in Orford-lane. Sho was then by herself, and said thathe,was going to Ground's shop for some kipper'. James Simister, 48, Forehaw-at., said that he found the body of the deceased at 11 30 on Sunday,in Bolton's Pit. A barbed wire fence suss round the pit, but it was broken in two places', for ab-ut four or five feet. The pit was about five feet deer', and the the slaps into it was rather steep. If the decavied had been blown in it was probable that the body w •nld have been found where it was, as the wind was blowing from Longford street.

Inspector Spinks said that sinne 1885 the following eight deaths had taken piece in the same place :—Aug. 18th, 1885, one woman, Gleahill ; gpril 18th, 1887, one man, Dykes; August 11th, 1889, one man, Richard Smith ; April 3rd, 1890, one girl. Littlefare ; May 21st, 1890. two men drowned, Forster and Watson: April 28 h, 1892, one man, Thomas Oldham, when the jury made a presentment that the pit he fenced off at once; aed the present case. In the prasenttnent of the jury in tbe case of Oldham, the jury recommended a sleeper fence.—The Coroner: Can you tell me how long the fence has beets in its present state ?—lt has been for some time, It. is difficult to keep the fences in that place-.—Do you know what has been done to do away with this death trap P—Ti•ev have been filling it up.— Is there not a notice on the ground stating that no more tipping will be allowed ?—That is so. Stanley Fair said that he was a clerk for Messrs. Bol- ton and Sons, who held the trace for Colonel Ireland Blackburne for 999 years. The firm required the water in the pit for their boilers. The pit was not more than five feet at the deepest part. It had been fenced round many a time, but the children round kept pulling the fence down. It was fenced with three rows of barbed wire about 15 inches apart.—The Coroner: Was it not suggested hy the jury at the last inquest that a sleeper fence should be put up ? Yes, it was.—lf thefence had hen put on the girl would not have . been blown in.— Mr. Fair : There is no right across the gronnd.—The Cor- oner. : I have already expressed my opinion upon that subject and I shall express myself again. What do you propose to do now that another death has taken place there ? We will fence it round again.—Row? Same as before —Why cannot you fill it up We require the water.—Why cannot you use the town's water? It is more ,xnenap.—What ia mnnev in comparison with loss of life. You must either fill the pit up and take the town's water, or rnu must allow the pit to remain, and allow other folks to he drowned, I should not hesitate What to do if I was the owner of the property, and had eeeh a presentment as was given at the last inquest. I should not have been able to have slept in my bed until the nit was filled up, and if another death had taken place, I should consider I was guilty of a crime. It was Owing to the bld fence that the girl has lost her life.—Mr. Fair: It is knocked down by people about.

—The Coroner : It is not the fence that the jury suggested.

Mr. Brighouse then asked the Town Clerk what he had to sac on behalf of the Warrington Corporation. Mr. Whittle said that the Corporation had been in negotia- tion with 'Messrs. Bolton and Sons for the purchase of the ground on which the pit stood, for the purpose of providing a recreation ground, but the conveyance had not yet been executed. As soon as the Corporation be- came owners of the land they would immediately fill up the pit. He expected they ix ould come in possession in about three weeks time.

The Coroner said that it would be a lamentable thing if fore those three week= expiri,d another case would have to be hlquired int.). Could not the Corporation and Messrs. Bolton awl Sons arranze matters so that the pit could be filled up the next morning, and thus begin the new year well ?

The Town Clerk said that he was prepared to do so if Messrs. Bolton and Sons would allow them to come upon the land. Mr. Fair promised that this should be done, and the pit be filled the next day. The Coroner then addressed the jury. He said that there was no evidence to show how the deceased got into the water, although it was probable that she had been blown in, and the verdict he would suggest was " found drowned." did not think it right to leave them without saying something about the loss of life which had takon place in that piece of water. Since 1885 seven persons had lost their lives,and now there was another. He thought that without trying to deal un- fairly with the matter, he must express the decided opinion that neither the Corporation of Warrington, nor the property owners ought to allow such dangerous places to remain in the h Tough. If he were the owner of that piece of water he would not keep the owners of adjacent houses in danger, hut would have the pit filled up. The town's water might be a little more expanse, but money could not compensate for loss of life. If loss of life occurred, he should have felt himself very much to blame. He thought it would strengthen the hands of the Town Clerk very much if he were allowed by Messrs. Bolton and one to enter the land the next day. The jury, after about twenty minutes deliberation.re- turned a verii,.t of" Found drowned," and also added a presentment that the pit ought to be filled up imme- diately. They were also ef opinion that had the pit been fenced as was recommended by the last jury, the present accident would not have happened. The Town Clerk and Mr. Fair promised that the pit should be fitted immediately.

THE ANNUAL VOLUNTEERS' BALL.

On Monday night the usual New Year's ball, pro- moted by the corntriiiisioned officers and yllunteers, was held in the Drill Shed. There were about 100 couples present, and the dancing was kept up with spirit until the early hours ofliTuesday morning to the strains of the Volunteer Band, under the con- ductorship of Bandmaster Armstrong. Bugler- Major Plinaton acted ea M.C. CAROL SERVICE ATTADGATE, On Saturday afternoon the, Rev. J. J. Lanham conducted a special carol service in the Chriist Church, Padgate. The members of the Church Lads' Brigade paraded at the residence of their Captain Hall, and marched headed by their bugle band to the church to attend the service. Captain Appleton was in command, and was accompanied by Lieutenants Blackwell and Granger. At the close of the service they re-assembled, and after a short menial were dismis.ed.

.• RAILWAY EMPLOYES' DINNBR. The employee of the Latchford section of the 1.4 and N. W. -Railway Company held their 10th annual dinner on Friday evening week at the ChPahire Cheese, Latchford. A very excellent dinner was provided by Mrs. Ireland. After dinner the gnests prepared themselves for a smoking concert in the large club room at which Mr. Warren,stationmaster presided. The Latchford quartette party, Messrs. R. Grounds, Ptarkey. Atkinson and Brownlow ren- dered very acceptably several glees, which were thoroughly appreciated. Songs were sung by Messrs. Lunt, Hunter. R. Grounds and Short. A very en- joyable evening WA4 brought to a conclusion with a capital ventriloquial entertainment by Mr. Alfred Grounds. Votes of thanks to the various performers conclud-d an enjoyable entertainment.

EMMANUEL CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR kqOCIETY. The Emmanuel Church Christian Endeavour Society held on New Year's Ere, in the Emmanuel schoolroom, a congregational social evening. A very pleasant evening was spent, the various non-' tributicns to the programme being appropriate to I the close of the old year and the beginning of the new. In the course of the proceedings, Miss Lees, the recording secretary of the Christian Endeavour Society, ori behalf of the members drew attention to the fact that it was ju,t three years since the Rev. E. T. Reed came amongst them as their minis- ter, and referred to that time as one which would be looked back to with feelings of thank‘ulness. She also referred to the great blessing which Mr. Reed bad been the means of bringing to the church in commencing a Christian Endeavour Society. She concluded by asking Mr. Reed to accept a gold hedge as a token of their great ap- preciation and esteem. Mr. Reed, in a few well- chosen words, expressed his heartfelt thanks for this thoughtful and aft?ctionate indication of their regard. At the conclusion of the friendly gather- ing they adjourned to the church for the watch- night service, which was of an impressive charac- ter.

ENTERTAINMENT IN ST. PAUL'S CHURCH

In the Infants' S,hool, IC-endrick-sir—et,- on Saturday evening.the penny entertainments in con- nection with St. Paul's Church were continued and an excellent programme was submitted, but there was only a poor audience. Mr. T. Morris presided, and expressed a hope that the Vicar and his wife would be able to get the assistance of the young men of the parish, to make these entertainments a success. He wished them every success. During the evening Mr. Morris asked two or three of the young men present to use their efforts to fill the room on the following Saturday. Th E EX A 'MINER,

SATURDAY, JANUARY 5, 1895.

Tr:um seems to be a great change Disunited unienists. coming over the position of the

Unionists in the North of Ireland. The self styled " loyalists " of Ulster are not now the happy family they used to be. Discord is creeping in between landlords and tenant farmers, and the once solid,phalanx of anti-Home-Rulers is showing serious signs of disintegration. The cry against Home Rule has hitherto admirably answered the purposes of the small class of land- lords in staving off social as well as political re- form. It has often been alleged that the real I cause of the agitation for self-government in Ire- land is the land question, but it would be much nearer the mark to say that the intense hostility to Home Rule of the landed class in that country is largely due to the fear of land reform. This fact the Protestant tenant farmers of Ulster are beginning to discover. They, like other agricul- turists,. have been seriously affected by the fall in prices of agricultural produce. But their claims for practical aid and redress are not listened to with a sympathetic ear by their land- lords. These latter gentlemen seem, on the whole, to be disposed te insist upon having their full pound of flesh in the way of rent. This un- sympathetic attitude is chilling the ardour of the tenants in their antagonism to Home Rule. In some parts of Ulster, in fact, the tenants show a disposition to make co mmon cause with the Nationalists in support of the present Govern- ment and its programme of land reform. At some of their meetings they have actually gone so far as to call upon their Tory members to support the Government in this respect, and at other meet- ings the chosen speakers have been Nationalist members. Mr. T. W. RUSSELL, the Unionist M.P. for South Tyrone, has been bitterly assailed by the landlord party for having dared to speak in Parliament on behalf of the Government's land policy, and he has even been threatened with ejectment from his seat. But the tenant farmers are rallying to his support, and even an Orange lodge has passed a resolution ap- proving of his conduct and promising to support him "no matter from what quarter the opposition may come." The Irish Unionists may well be alarmed at these signs of the times. Dr. RENTOUL, who is well known to Warrington Tories,gees so far as to complain in the chief Tory paper of Belfast that he has not seen the report of a single meet- ing at which an attack was mot made on 801110 Unionist and "no attack whatever made on any Rome Ruler." It is now fully expected that a few more seats will pass into Nationalist hands at the next election, and, indeed, the next election may possibly see the complete break-up of the " solid North."

A writer in the "Manchester The Coal and fres Trades. Guardian who deals exhaus- tively with his subject, gives a rather gloomy account of the Lancashire coal and iron trades during 1894. In both trades business has, taking the year through, bees unsatisfactory. It was anticipated that the great cessation of industry caused by the dispute between colliers and coalowners last year, would have given a fillip to trade for a few months at all events. But these anticipations were not realised, and, as far as the coal trade is concerned, the writer suggests that the position has become even worse than it was before the great stoppage. A strong attempt was made, chiefly by Lancashire coalowners, to repress the wild competition which reduces prices to an unprofitable point, but it did not succeed. The combined coalowners were undersold by those who remained outside the ring and by competitors in other counties. One coal- owner after another seceded from the movement, and, of course, with every secession it became weaker and less able to effect its object, and it was finally abandoned. In the presence of a depressed market this combination was almost bound to fail. A section of coal owners—even a powerful section—could not regulate competition, and the assistance of the Miners Federation,which could have exerted a more extensive influence, does not seem to have been sought. The strike of Scotch miners improved the demand i for English coal, but not to the extent that might

,•,

d:tj

have been expected, and with the collapse of that strike in September the trade was again in as deep a " slough " as ever. The exceptionally mild winter has been another factor in the depression, and, altogether, the year closes with but a poor record in the coal trade. In the iron trade, too, we are told that prices have ruled low, and that all the hopes of improvement have been falsified. The trade has, it is true, had its fluctuations though not on a large scale, but the market has, it is said, steadily weakened. There is, however, one circumstance which is noted, and which seems to afford some ray of hope for the coming year. It s reported that while the Amalgamated Society of Engineers began the year in the Manchester district with twenty per cent, of its members in receipt of donation benefit, the number is now about ten per cent., and is showing a steady de- crease. Of course the twenty per cent, at the be- ginning of the year was largely due to the usual temporary suspensions for the holidays, but at the same time the fact that there is a steady de- crease of out-of-work members in this large and representative trades union at a time of the year when the tendency is generally the other way should not be ignored. It may possibly indicate that revival of trade which is ardently wished for.

IT is stated that about three thous- The Ship Canal. and vessels have entered the East- ham locks during the twelve months that the Ship Canal has been open. °f these jut over five hundred have been foreign vessels bound for Manchester. The first ail months' traffic was disappointing, but the pros- pects have brightened very considerably in the latter half of the year, aid it is expected that the next few weeks will see a further enlargement of the trade of the Canal. The report for the quar- ter ending October 31, 1894, of Mr. HILL' the Manchester Corporation engineer, which has been published this week, is satisfac- tory as far as it goes, though it does riot cover much ground. The days of reckless uncou trolled expenditure are evidently at an end. Mr. HILL reports that great care and economy are exercised over the expenditure with the result that there is a larger balance in hand. On the 30th June the unappropriated balance %co £91,807 but this has now been increased by the sale of plant and in other ways to £138,771 which can be expended as circumstances require. The year upon which we have just entered will be crucial one in the history of the Canal. If the upward tendency of traffic which has lately been witnessed steadily continues, it will be very fair evidence that the great waterway is to be a success ; but if, on the other hand, it prove to be but a delusive spurt,the friends of the Canal, and the investors in it, may well be apprehensive of what the future has in store for it.

Mr. Gransrown has lost none Of Eastern Atrocities. his old'fire when denouncing wrong and cruelty. A deputation of Armenians made a presentation to him onSatur- day on the occasion of his eighty-fifth birthday) and he took the opportunity to refer to the recent revelations of outrages on defenceless Armenians by the " unspeakable " Turks. It is close upon twenty years ago that Mr. GLADSTONE roused the the indignation of Englishmen at the outrages upon Bulgarians, and there is, as he says, only too much reason to fear that "the outrages and sins and abominations" of 1876 have within the . last few months been repeated in Armenia. If it be so,—if half the stories of hideous outrage that have recently been told be true—there are few people who will not agree with Mr. GLADSTONE that it is time. "one general shout of execration, not of men but of deeds, one general shout of execration directed against deeds of wickedness, shouid rise from out- raged humanity, and should force itself into the ears of the SULTAN of Twarcxy and make him sensible, if anything could make him sensible, of the madness of smith a course." It is to be feared that the Turk is irreclaimable, and that there win be no peace or decent government in the countries - over which he has dominion till he has been deposed from his place of power by a more civilised and humane nation.

ODDF.ELLOWSHIP IN RUNCORN DISTRICT.. ..

The Runcorn district branch of Oddfel tows held their annual meeting at the Royal Oak Lodge, Widnes. P Tansey occupied the chair; Bro. W. Joynson occupying the deputy-chair. The return of membership submitted showed that there had been an inerea-e during the past twelve months.
