THE NEW TRADES' HALL AT RUN CORN. _- MR. BRUNNER'S MUNIFICENCE.

THE OPENING. CEREMONY.

New Year's Day was a red letter day in the history of Runcorn Trades' Unionism, when Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., opened the New Trades' Hall in John-street, which he has generously presented for the benefit of the Trades' Unions and Friendly Societies in Runcorn. A portion of the building comprises what formerly was the Presby- terian Chapel, but which has now been converted into a commodious and most c mafortable meeting room. Adjoining this room, a new building has been erected in the Queen Anne stlle. I is built of red brick with Rub on dressing, and Yorkshire stone. On the ground floor there are five club rooms, offices, and other con- veniences. On the second floor, which is reached by a stone staircase, there is a large assembly room suitable , for tea meetings, etc. By a well-adapree system of moveable partitions, this room could, if desired, be , transformed into five rooms, for the purposes of corn- ! mittee, and other meetings. The building throughout is I well lighted, heated, and ventilated. Mr. J. Bilfington, the architect and cleik of works, and the contractor, Mr. W. Nixon are to be congratulated upon the result of their efforts. The total cost which has been defrayed b) Mr. Brunner, is £2,000. Great preparations bad been made for the opening ceremony. There was a profuse display of bunting at the hall, and outside, near the entrance, an attractive triumphal arch had been erected. In the centre of the arch were the words " Suecess to Mr. Brunner" whilst underneath" Capital and Labour" were represented to be shaking hands in friendship. Mr. Brunner was met at the railway station, a little before two o'clock, by a number of the members of the new Urban Council, members of Trades' Unions in the town, and others.

A PROCESSION', was at once formed and proceeded through the principal streets leading to the new hall. The procession was headed by the Independent Order of Recha bites pioneer temperance brass hand of Manchester. followed by the members of the Upper Mersey Watermen and Porters' Association, with their banner. They were accompanied by Mr. Billane, president. of the Watermen'm Union, and Mr. J. Banner, vice-president. Members of the Brick- layer's Association walked next, and these were snc- ceeded by the Runcorn and district brass band. There were also in the procession members of the following trades' unions with their respective officials :—Ship- wrights, Mr Neil (Mersey district delegate, Liverpool), Mr. Henry Dale (local secretary). Mr. John Taylor (general secretary), Mr. George Kirkham, D.C. (presi- dent). Mr. James Sales (Shipwright's representative on the Trades' Council), and Mr. John Gresty (marshall). Bakers accompanied by Mr. Samuel Hughes (chairman of the local branch), and Mr. Wm. Burton (secretary), Society of carpenters and joiners headed by Mr. W. Tun- lay (organiser of Burton-on-Trent). Mr. E. Aston (secre- tary), Mr. John Williams (treasurer) and Mr. Billington (marshal!). Boilermakers with their president, Mr. Nathaniel I Lightfoot, and secretary and treasurer, Messrs. S. Blundell and. Wm. Ouse, respectively. En- gineers and crane men accompanied by Mr. Henry Brough (Manchester), John Ormrod (president of the local branch), Matthew Gitteus (treasurer), and S. Mg- net (secretary). Painters, including Mr. Wm. Curving (president), Mr. David Pillington (secretary), Mr. James Campbell (assistant secretary),Mr. F. Bishop (treasurer). and Messrs. Hoxworth and Oliver (stewards). Several of the societirs carried their banners and emblems,whilst the bakers also displayed a huge loaf of bread. About the centre of the procession there was a waggonette, drawn by a couple of horses, containing Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P.. Mr. 'T. Snape, M.P. for the Heywood divi,inn. Mr. E. Mnrshall (secretary District Council), and Mr. E. Gatley (secretary Watermen's Association). On his ARRIVAL 'KT THE TRADES' HALL . .

Mr. Brunner was presented by Mr. J. Billington with a key to open the hall door. In doing an, Mr. Billington expressed the deep gratitude of the trades'unionista of the town for Mr. Brunner's great kindness to them,and said they would use their utmost endeavour not to misplace con-Mance he had reposed in them.—Mr.Brunner said the he was very glad indeed to perform the small ceremony of opening the door which would give access to a build- ing he honed, in their hands, would do very great good in the future. That building, as they knew,was intended for the use of trades' societies and friendly societies in Runcorn, and in it he hoped many a meeting would be held for their benefit • for the benefit of their wives. and children, and for those who came after them. He opened that door, and bade them welcome as the real owners of the building. (Loud applause.) After an inspection of the building had been made, a LARGELY ATTENDED MEETING was held in the meeting room of the hall. The chair Was occupied by Mr. George Kirkham, and he was sup Ported on the platform by Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., Mr. T. Snapp, M.P., Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., Mr. Philip Speak- man, J P., C.C.. Mr. J. W. Handley, Fisher, Hankinson and others.—The Chairman called upon Mr. Gatley to make a statemeat with refer- ence to the erection of the hall. Mr. Galley said it gave him great pleasure to see so many friends around them on an occasion like that. Through their approaching Mr. Brunner to ask for his assistance in the erection of a hall, they had now a hgme for the whole of the trades' and friendly societies in the town in which they could meet, converse, and enjoy themselves. They had overcome many difficulties, but they had still many difficulties to overcome. Mr. Brun- ner had not only been a friend to their trades' societies but to other societies in the town. He felt very proud upon that occasion to thank Mr. Brunner for all he had done for them. He hoped the workingmen of Runcorn would assist in making the hall useful and successful. He hoped they would have the pleasure of meeting Mr. Brunner in it from time to time ; and that they as workingmen might work together for the benefit of each other. (Applause.) MR. J. T. DRUMMER. who on rising to speak, was received with loud and pro- longed cheering, said Mr. Ellis Godley and he ha.l neen in communication with each other for some years for the purpose of providing a meeting place which should be- long to the working people of Runcorn,and in which they could band thensselyes together for their own protection for the protection of their families, and making provi- sion for old age. (Applause.) He had thought a year or two age of a building in another part of the town, but as the present one was in the market, and suitable for the purpose they had in view' and especially when he heard that the purchase of it would he an aid to a congregation of people engaged in a very good work, he decided to purchase it. He sold the other building to another flourishing institution which he need not, fur- ther particularise. The congregstinn which had pre- viously occupied that building had moved away,and he believed was very much better housed than it was he- fore. He was sure they would Join with him in wish. lag them prosperity. There had been sransider- able difficulties to be met, regarding that budding, and 1 he did not see that they had been all met. One of the difficulties was this, that none of them could become quite sure that any of the inatitutiens or societies for which the building was henceforward to be devoted, could be looked upon to be permanent societies. He wanted to provide A PERMANENT OWNER TO THE NOILDING. tie that if ever it failed to be used for what it was at present intended, and was liable to fall into rack and ruin, it might be used for the benefit of the public in some other way. It was for that reason that he had in- vited Mr. Taylor, chairman of the Board of Commis- sioners, to attend there that day in order that he, or hie successor, the chief magistrate or chief citiz-n of Run- corn, should be the owner of the freehold, and trustee for the working people of the town and neighbourhood. (Applause.) That had been one of his Mr:tildes and that was the way he had attempted to get over it. HP was told there was a number of the new urban district councillors present, and he was very glad indeed to meet them on that—their first day f office and on an occasion an pleasant. He wished them a Happy New Year" and, also many years of usefulness to he people of Ruin. corn. That building was to be used for the furtherance of the business of the trades' societies, and he hoped by and by for ftieudly societies. He did not think they were unworthy succesaers to a religious congregation. The work of the triades'societiem and of the friendly socie- ties was animated by brotherly love. (Applause.) They

===== 0002601_18950105_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
CONVIVIALITY APART PROM BUSINESS.

He did not stand there as an eiaemy of conviviality. He liked joviality when in its proper place. He heartily ra commended to all the societies of Runcorn that build- ing, and asked then, to make use of it, regarding it as their own, in which to transact their business apart from a temptation to which so many of them met with. It had been a matter of distress L. him, that even little children, from their earliest years, should become ac- customed to sights which all decent fathers and mothers should keep them away from, if it was possible to do so. Children were sent with monetary contributions to the various societies, and he asked their parents to do their best in order that these children should be able to collie into a place that was consecrated to noble ideas, and be kept away from one of the saddest of all temptations that came before them. (Applause.) It was well that thov should keep business and conviviality apart, and Ile saw from time to time, evidence, that the majority of working-men agreed with him upon this point. They had decided that no political work should be carried on in licensed premises, and also that 'parish council meet- ings should not be held in them. They also saw that local authorities were providing places for the holdingof inquests, in order to keep them away from licensed pre- mises. Let men who could restrain themselves go to licensed premises, but let them keep their business apart from those places. (Applause.) There had been, so far nine trades' societies in the town who had decided that they would take a share in whatever benefits could be derived from that building. So far, no friendly societies had come into the scheme. He heartily invited them to take advantage of the building. (Applause.) He was told by Mr. Billington, a working joiner, had been the architect of the building, which from its large conception to its smallest detail did him a vast amount of credit, and there was room for every trade society and every friendly society in Runcorn. The more that joined in the use of the building, the smaller would the expense be for each. It was his wish, and the commit- tee joined with him in the wish, that NO RENT SHOULD BE CHARGED . .

for the building. (Applause.) The expense to the societies would be limited to cleaning, lighting,warming and a sufficient amount for repairs to maintain the building in good condition, It was their buildii,g and all they were called upon to do was to pay sufficient money to maintain it in proper order. (Applause.) The management should be in the hands of a committee selected by the societies using the building, and they should pledge themselves to find a sufficient amount of annual income to pay the expenses he had detailed. (Applause.) At the close of the meeting he hoped to have a friendly conference with the committee which had been formed, and also with representatives of , friendly societies, and members of the Urban District I Council with reference to the trust deed f the building. , He heartily wished that it might be a blessing to them, and if they would permit him, he gave it with the hope that they would, by their own manly exercise, make it not only of benefit to themselves but to their families and children. (Applause.)

THANKS.

The Chairman, in calling npon Mr. Taylor to speak on behalf of the District Council, said they as Trades' Unionists, and he thought also, the friendly societies, would only be too glad to take the matter up. If they could not find sufficient funds to support it, it would be shame on their part. He thanked Mr. Brunner most heartily on behalf of the Trades' Unionists.—Mr. Taylor also thanked Mr.Brunner on behalf of the District Coun- cil. He said he was sure the gift would be appreciated , by those who participated in it. That was not the only occasion that the people of Runcorn had been thankful ' to Mr. Brunner. He had helped Runcorn ti a very great extent. They had had no object which had been 1 for the welfare of the town, and the benefit and interest 1 of the masses which Mr. Brunner had net given his practical sympathy and help to. The building of their Technical Institute could not have been proceeded with had it not been for Mr. Brunner's great practical help. They had been enabled to erect a building which he might say would in time to come, be appreciated by every individual in Runcorn. He (the speaker) was very much pleased, the previous week, when the secre- tary to the distress fund (Mr. Marshall) informed him that Mr. Brunner had written to him offering £2O per week during the time the distress existed in the town. (Applause.) Since they had had Mr. Brunner to repre- sent them in Parliament he had taken great interest in all questions which had come forward in Parliament affecting the people of Riancorn. He thanked him sin- cerely for all his past kindness,and hoped that the build- ing would be used in a proper spirit, and that it would become a blessing to those who used it. MR. SNAPS'S ADDRESS.

Mr. Snape, Who met with a most hearty recep- tion, said he must first express his amazement at the temerity ot Mr. Brunner and himself for venturing before that audience. (Laughter.) They had been told on the authority of one of the most eminent political personages of the day that trades' unions were cruel organisatioes. His amazement was increased when Mr. Brunner had had the courage to provide that building in order that they might strengthen their trades' unions by meeting together, and thus be strengthening their cruel organisations. (Laughter.) The welcome they had given to Mr. Brunner assured him that they need not dread any terrible consequences. He had seen, dur- ing his life time, the advantages of trades' unions upon the members and upon the woi king classes generally, and this w staid bsnish scepticism from his mind with regard to trades unionism, if cver it ventured to find a lurking place there. He thought they could not but acknowledge bow grateful the community of Runcorn ought to be to Mr. Brunner for having such thoughtful consideration for the working-classes by giving them that place of meeting. He thought it was very desirable that their meetings ehetild be held in a place in which their minds would be free to attend to the business they were met to transact. (Hear, hear.) When that bene- ficent piece of legislation, the Parish Councils Bill, was under consideration in the House of Commons, a clause was introduced that whenever possible the meetings should he held in the schoolroom of the parish. When , the Bill get into another place, the lords temporal and the lords spiritual, did their best to bring the Parish Council into the public-house. But he was pleased to say that when the Bill Came back to the House of Com- mons they declined to let it pass, and they ultimately succeeded in reinstating the clause, so that the peeps could hold their meetings in the schools, which were supported by public funds. If that was good for the Parish Councils.it was good for trades' unions. He claimed for himself, and he was sure he could claim it for Mt. Brunner, that they were heart and soul "

IN SYMPATHY WITH LABOUR. They were not the only members interested in Labour They had some splendid men in the House of Commons who were sent there by the working classes. (Applause). The labour members sat on the same side of the Hot/se as the Liberal members, and they might take it, that the Government which also sat on the same side of tke House, was a government which was endeavouring to promote the interests of the working classes Bo far as legislation would offer. They had done that in various ways since he (the speaker) had been a member of the House of Commons. They had reduced the hours of labour in the government workshops to eight hours per day. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Brunner had set them a noble example previously in his own works tapplause), and men like Mr. Brunner, and Mr. William Mather, of Sal- ford, were able to give the government a little light and leading upon that question. If in their muni- cipality, or district council, they had persons In their employ who were paid out of the public rates, they should make the conditions and terms of the most exem- plary character in order that they raight point private employers to the way they conducted public employ- ment. They knew that the government introduced,and passed through the MUM, of Commons a bill which was on the whole a very satisfactory ono. But when that got to

Tee Verse NOOSE they inserted the contracting out clause, which was fatal to the bill. They were just in this position. They had an authority which called itself umpire, but which always gave its own side " in "and at the other side " out" whether they were or not (laughter.) Work which had cost the members of the House of Commons weary nights, weeks, and months, was undone in a day or two in "another place." In the interests of the Tory workingman as well as the interests of the Radical work- ingman, they would have to get rid of this destructive force which existed in the House of Lords. (Hear, hear.) They were sometimes told by the members of the Inde- pendent Labour Party that they did not do as much as they ought to do, but he could say that the present'gev- ernment had done mere than had ever been done in any

• previous Parliament on behalf of la hour,and they would have done a great deal more, if it had not been for that ante-deluvian institution, the House of Lords. Be re- joiced in the opening of that ball and with the PROGRESS OF TRADES' UNIONS. They knew how for the last 20 years the prices of menu- factured articles had been falling, and that to-day they were at as low an ebb as they had ever been known in the history of the land, and yet while that had been the case, the wages of the working classes had been on the whole fully maintained and in some instances increased. The purchasing power of their wages was greater to-day than previously. He held that had it not been for the solid trades' unions of the working-classes, employers would have reduced wages, and the working-classes would have been in a much more unsatisfactory position. The capitalists had not been making, as largo returns as formerly, but the working-classes had been getting a larger share, and thus had been brought into operation what ought to he the desire of every true heart—a more equal division of wealth. (Applause.) He was IN FAVOUR OF A LIVING WAGE. He did not mean a crust of dry bread, and a drink of water—although he supposed a man could exist on that. He would call that a starvation wage. A living wage should do something more than provide the necessaries of life, it should also provide some of the comforts and refinements of life, to enable a man to make his home a

OLD AGE PENSIONS

and he should like, if they could see a practical method, of having it settled. He did not think it was satisfac- tory to them to have one in three or five, of those whe had passed the age of 65, spending the remainder of their days in a workhouse. If any means could Ise devised by which the state could provide for these people, he for one, would rejoice in it, and try to brio! about a solution. He thought he trades' unions could help in that matter. (Hear, hear.) Their help had been sought, but they had not been able to rely on the pro- posals that had come from Birmingham. He also thought that the friendly societies should give attention to the subject and in that way endeavour to settle the question. (Applause.) In conclusion he said he had been intimately connected for nearly 40 years with Run- corn and Widnes, and he always felt that when he was amongst Runcorn or Widnes audiences he was once more at home. (Loud applause.)

TOTES OF THANKS.

Mr. Aston, as a trades' unionist, proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Brunner and Mr. Snaps for their ad- dresses, and in doing so spoke of some of the advantag, I of trades unions and friendly societies.—Mr. P. Speak- man, J.P., C.C., seconded, and said the presentation of the building to the trades' societies and friendly socie- ties of the town, was a very noble gift. Mr. Brunner had shown his great sympathy with them not only in their prosperity but also in their edversity. It was very nice when the societies alluded to, had a building like that in which to meet together and to discuss matters for the benefit of each other, and also for the benefit of the community. (Applause.) The vote was supported by Mr. Billam, president of the Watermen's Association; Mr. Tanal. delegate of the associated carpenters and joiners ; and Mr. Neil, delegate of the associated ship- wrights.—Mr. Brunner in reply on behalf of himself and Mr. Snaps thanked the audience for their kindly recep- tion. He said the District Council would incur no res- ponsibility in accepting the trust deed. It would be their right and their duty, when they saw that the hall was not going to fulfil the duties for which it was devoted, or that there was a risk of charge to the public in consequence of misuse of the building, to sell it for the good of the public.—Mr. Imisnn in a short speech proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman, alluding to the fact that that gentleman had been elected a member of the Urban District Council. tie always censidered that the District Council would not be complete unless there were a certain proportion of workingmen repre- sentatives upon it. Questions would come before the Council in which the interests of the trades in the town were deeply involved. Mr. Kirkham would be able to voice the opinions of the trades unionists in an intelli- gent manner. (Applause.)—Mr. Thomas Sayle seconded, and the Tote was passed unanimously.

===== 0002601_18950105_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONFERENCE.

A conference of the trades unionists, urban councillors and representatives of friendly societies was afterwards held in one of the rooms. Mr. S. Taylor occupied the chair. Mr. Brunner read a draft trust deed, after which there was a little discussion. Mr. Lightburn considered that it would be better for the matter to be discussed by the Urban Council, and the: they could bring their proposals to the trades' and friendly societies. They could then be further discussed. This was tacitly agreed to.

BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH,

Itt7INCOItN.

The annual congregational tea meeting in connection with this church was held in ths schoolroom on Wednes- day evening. A good number sat down to tea. The after proceedings were presided over by the Rev. J. S. Simpson, pastor of the church. The reports of the different organizations were submitted,and showed that the work carried on in conneccion with tt e church dur- ing the past year had been very successful. The secre- tary (Mr. Holt) in presenting his amaual report said r that in reviewing the church's work during the past I year, they had all reason to congratulate themselves, for altp.ist every organization in connection there with had been vi v successful. The church services had been well attended and much good had been done, which was shown by the substantial increase of the mem- bers' roll which now stood as follows :—num- her of members on church roll, year enclitic December, 1893, 158, losses by death and removal, 7, added by profe-asinn during the year, 26, giving a total membership of 177. That made the numoer added since Mr. Simpson's settlement, 130. The average attendance at the sacrament had been 68, against 62, during 1893. Regarding the musical portion of the services he was pleased to say the choir was much better organized than when he eave his last report. In the bringing about of that he was sure they all recognized the ability displayed by their organist who had devoted much time and energy to that department. The week night services had been well attended. The pastor had, during the year, formed a literary and social society which had about 30 members, and had also commenced a Christian Endeavour class, in which great interest had been taken. The class had about 50 MM.. hers. Mr. Frimpson had also formed a children's class, which had been a great success. The work of the past year promised well for the future suc- cess of the church —The Treasurer of the church sub- mitted the financial statement which was considered .ery satisfactory. He also gave the report of the Bethesda Sunday school. Mr. Peter Cartwright pre- sented a report of St. .Luke's Sunday school, which showed that excellent work was being accomplished. Mr. Le Couteur was voted an honorarium for his ser- vices as organist and choirmaster.

THINGS HEAIZ, AND SEEN AT RUl\ C /RN

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

"The old 'meth place to the new,' and simul- taneously with the departure of the old year, the Runcorn Board of Commissioners finished its course. The last meeting was held on Friday, and the new Urban District Council, which supersedes it, may be said to have taken up its duties in real earnest at its first meeting yesterday morning. The most important business at the final meeting of the Commissioners was the proposal submltted and agreed to that application be made for the consoli- dation of two loans of £l,BOO and £5,400. The former suns is a loan of tong standing, and the Local Government Board had written to the Autho- rity stating that it should have been paid off, years ago. The Commissioners were, therefore, obliged to take some steps for liquidating the loan. To pay off the Wm at once would have required an additional rate of about 81. or 91. in the X. This, under present circumstances, was impractic- able, so the Commissioners decided to take advan- tage of a counter proposal, for the consolidation of the two sums mentoned, which will enable them to wipe off the two amounts in 15 years. It was satisfactory to know that with the exception of the ! water works and street improvement loans, the indebtednese of the town for buildings and numer- ous things in their possession' only amounts to £13,200. At the conclusion of the meeting a well- deserved compliment was paid to Mr. Taylor for the efficient way he had performed his duties as chair- man. _ .

, The proceedings of the new Urban Council on Thursday. were characterized by a remarkable unanimity. Tne chairmanship of the Council, for wnich it was thought there woald be two or three members proposed, was deAded unanimously., Mr. E. Imison who was appointed to the honourable position, which also carries with it the title of J.P., is to be congratulated upon having the confilence of the members who previously sat with him on the old Board of Commissioners, and also the new members. There was also an absence of any friction in the election of vice-chairman ; in fact, the two positions seemed to have been practically decided upon at the private meeting of the mem- bers which was held the previous night. A slight change was made in the personnel of the com- mittees. Instead oi several members comprising certain committees, the whole council will co oprise each committee with the exception of the Free Library Committee, and the Technical Instruction Committee. _ _

A somewhat novel procedure was taken for determining the length of office for the councillors representing Mersey ward, who, it will be remem- bered, were lucky enough to escape a contest. Tile result of the " draw" by the Chairman for the positions entitle Mr. Kirkham to it on the Council for three years, Mr. Once for two, and Mr. Posnett for one only. No doubt, Mr. Kirkham will be far- more satisfied with tbe result of the drawing than Mr. Po3nett. _

If. evidence were nee.ied of the munificence of Mr Brunner, M.P., io the town of Runcorn, it.was forthcoming on New Year's Day when he formally opened the New Trad,s 11111, which he has pre - eented at a cost of .£2,000 to the trades unions and friendly societies in the town. The hall is intended tor the holding of the numerous meetings held in connection with these societies. At present the meeting of the trades' societies and friendly societies are, as a rule, held, through lack of more suitable accommodation, in public-houses, which, as was stated, tend to distract the members from the business they have in hand,and are also a source of temptation, especially to children of various ages, who are often sent with monetary subscriptions to the lodges or societies. The Trades' Ball will pro- vide the societies of Runcorn with a more suitable meeting place. It is a most suitable and comfort- ably arranged building, and will accommodate the whole of the societies it is meant to benefit. As no rent is to be charged for the use of Vie building, the only expenses being the cost of maintenance,it ICI ay be expected that full advantage will be taken of the building by the various societies in the town.

THE MIDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT:

Dr. McDougall's report stated that the number of deaths registered during the past month amounts to 2. There were seven more inquest eases, and f the re- mainine 21 two were due to premature birth, two to senile decay, four to pneumonia, four to diarrhoea, two to tubercular disease, and one each to inflammation of the wind pipe, paralysis. heart diaeass, enteric fever debility, bronchitis and cancer. The 28 deaths showed an average death-rate for the twelve months of 16.8,and exclusive of the inquest cases 126 of the estimated population. Forty-seven deaths (five inquests) were registered during the corresponding month of last year. The report was considered very satisfactory. This concluded the business.

NOT4S BY THE WAY AT EARLE,STOWN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

The old Board of Newton Improvement Commis- sioners have dissolved partnerships, ail after a general stroke down, they eaen departed on their several ways, some to re-assemble later on under a different name, others to hide their lights under a bushel. There has been a general sweeping out,. and dusting of old corners, so that when the brand new Councillors meet they will find everything spick and span,ancl a golden opportunity to make a new start in life. I hope, however, no spasmodic attempt at re-adjustment will be made,but that, our new governors will take example from Use old hands, and study carefully the course of events before launching out in any new ventures. lam not going to deny for a moment that some matters are urgent, and need immediate attention, but it would be well to lay to heart the wise old saw, " Look before you leap." 01' course, different sec- tions of the people have their own peculiar fads, and possibly each individual section would like its particular fad to receive first place in the attention of the Councillors. but " patience' must be the war cry.

The first meeting of the new Council isto be held Monday afternoon next, and I suppose at this gathering the two important questions to be decided will be the election ot chairmen, and the day and hour of the future general meetings of the Council. On the former question I have already given my views, and tke latter I suppose the Councillors will say, is their business. Well, it may be, and it is also the busine.s of the public too, for according to the Act the public ha% ea richt of admission to the monthly meetings ot tie o,uncil. But supposing the meetings are held in the middle of the after- noon, unless it be Saturday afternoon, what earthly chance will the public have of being present, especially working-men, in whose behalf the Act was principally framed. This however is a minor point, the chief point being whether the hour of meeting will be SO fixed, as to make it practicable tor working-men who are members of the Council to attend the meetings without sacrificing any por- tion of their hard earned weekly wage. Whrn a working-man is willing to devote himself to the service of his fellowmen, in this way, it is hardly fair that we should expect him to make a monetary sacrifice on our behalf as well. Therefore seeing that the Council is a democratic body, it should meet at a democratic hour, when working-men have finished their daily toil, as the Act really intended.

I wonder what local surprises the new year has in store for us, or what improvements are likely to be pushed forward. It is all "off," with the skating rink, but I wonder whether the public baths will become an accomplished tact. Of course the new Council by adopting the "public baths and wash- houses" Act, can supply us with a good plunge bath now, without any difficulty. Will the vexed night soil question be " removed off the face of the earth, never to be seen again ' (vide address) by Isome "crematory " process? Shall we be possessed before Dec. 31st ef a fire engine and amateur lire brigade? It it probable that we shall be able to wander along our main streets, shortly, without wallowing in inches of mud I' Do you think we shall be able to get an independent water Supply and retail it at less than sixpence per 1000 gallons? The market ground is being relaid with cinders &c., is it possible it will ever be covered in, and thus obviate a repetition of last Friday night's experience, when most of the stalls were over- turned by the storm? These are only some of the grand innovations promised, when the councillors were candidate.. What an interesting study to. watch the realisation of these promises?

They tell me that the distress and misery at pre- sent existing in llaydock is the keenest that has been known for a number of years. In fact dur- ing the great strikes which have prevailed in the coal trade, the people have never been so impo- verished as they are at present. It would need more space than you could allow, were Ito re- count the heartrending cases which have come under my notice this festive season. One instance I may m-ntion, a respectable hardworking collier,. a couple of weeks ago, after paying his drawer and other contributions had to take home, of the result of his labours the paltry sum of threepence, and on this, had to maintain a wife and five children. The poor fellow was so put about, that he actually act by the roadside and wept. This is only one eve among scorEs, but it is a sure indication, of the ex- tremely bad condition of trade. Thousands of t,ms of coal and slack is being stacked on the pit banks, and when this is the case it is pretty evident that ths poor fellows who labour below cannot be any- thing like fully employed.

The revenue returns relating to the period between April let and December Met show a net increase of re- venuei of £2,839,136. Property and income te X' showed Rn neres‘c ol £428,000, damns £1,074,029, Customs of £340,245, and Excise of £790,074. Wintry and stormy weather prevailed on Monday in many parts of the country. In some districts there were heavs, snowfalls, and in tho North of Scotland the drifts were so deep that several railway tracks were blocked. Consols (two and three-quarters per cent.) on Monday closed at 1031—the highest price ever reached by British funds.

BOW TO DEFEAT OU It ENEMIES.

Everything that tends to weaken our constitutinn ano render it liable to disease is a' enemy of mankind, and everything that tends to withstand the attacks of thew enemies and to restore health and strength to the human system is a tree friend. Among the most prominent. in most dreadful. like so many gene. als in the attacking °Mos of the great enemy a mankind— hcith—miv be mentioned the following :—lndiyestion, liver cotnplaint, nervous disorders. and chest affec,ians, while the diff r- ent symptom., by which thea, diseases manifest them selves may be regarded as so many sub-officers in the ranks of the enemy. When any one of these attacks u, we may be certa.n that some of he others are close at • hand. The stomach, liver, lungs, nerves, arid blood, are portions of the body that the enemy is very urone to assail, and iflo succeeds in maintaining his p•asition for any length or time in any one of them, and is not quickly expelled, he soon extends his pernicious domin- ions to other parts of the body. -- TRAvELLING WITHOUT TICKET.—EXEMPLARY SENTENce. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, yesterday, before Dr. Gornall (chairman) and Mr. J. H. Fleming, Wm. Landon was charged with having on 28'h Nov. travelled on the London and North-Western railway from Acton Bridge to Warrington without having peid his fare. Defendant pleaded guilty.—Mr. Fenna, solici- tor, appeared for the company, and said the defendant . was formerly a clerk in the compaey's service, and on the 28th of last month he was seen to enter Bank Quay station with another man who took a ticket for one, but defendant did not take nue. At tae high level Tiletfortn both got into the 5.15 train to Crews, and the oak:els' suspicions being aroused at Warrington, word was sent to Crewe with the result that defendant was seen to get out of the train there. He got on a return train • later on, and at Warrington was seen to get over the wall of the station into Factory-lane. The ticket collec ter gave chase, but was unable to catch defendant. Though charged with travelling without, ticket from ' Acton Bridge it was proved that defendant had really come from Crewe.—James Shaw, porter at Bank Quay, deposed to seeing the defendant on the date in question get into the Crewe train and also saw him get out of another train arriving from that town at 844.— William Miller, guard of the train, also saw the defend- ant get out at Crewe and return to Warrington.—The Bench considered the offence a serious one, and fined defendant 20s. and costs (£2 03. 6.1. in all). He paid before leaving the Court. A. PUBLICAN Daum( ON His Own PREMISES. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Samuel Jones, "Smith's Arms," Legh-st., was charged with being drunk on his own licensed premises on the 24th December.—Defend- ant pleaded guilty.—Detective Inspector Spinks said that on Monday, 24th of December. P.C. Evers gave him some information which led to his going to defendant's house at 9.25. Hearing a noise, he went inside and found the defeedant behind the bar,drunk. He remon- strated with him and asked him to go upstairs and not make any more noise. Defenlant said he was not drunk, and followed witness out into the street. Shortly after- wards, hearing the disorder commencing again he went in and found defendant attempting to strike his wife and making use of very foul language.—Defendant said "1 had a drop too much beer and I own to it." His wife and he were not quarrelling but he had spoken to her that way because he was vexed.—Inspector Spinks said Mrs. Jones had conducted herself properly.—The Bench fined defendant sa. and costs. THIFT FROM A POTTERY. On Thursday morning at the Borough Police Court, Amy Harry, 42, Warburton-street, and Ann Floyd, 31, Warburton-street, were both charged with stealing from the pottery of Janses Grace, Winwick-road, on Wednes- day evening, two mugs, value is. 93.' or receiving them knowing them to have been stolen. Mr. Hunt (chief constable) said that on Wednesday evening. D.S. Monaghan was in Horsemarket-st. about seven o'clock, when he saw Floyd with something bulky under her shawl. He followed her to her daughter's house in Rose and Crown-street, and was in time to see her take a mug from under her shawl and put it down. On being questioned she said she had brought the mug from a woman .named Harris. She was brought down to the police station and then DS. Monaghan and D.I. Spinks went to Floyd's house and found the second mug, which had been brought by Harris who was apprehended. From other evidence produced it appeared that Harris had bargained with Floyd to get her a mug if she would give her (Harris) some food. Floyd did this under the impression that the money for the mugs would be stopped cut of Harris's wage.—Mr. J. Grace identi- fied the two mugs as his property, and said that Harris bad no right to take them. He wished that this system of petty thieving were put a stop to, as he had missed several articles of a like nature at different times. Two witnesses supported the statement that Harris bar- gained with Floyd in the terms descrihed.—The Bench sent Barris to gaol for 14 days' hard labour and Floyd was acquitted. ANOTHER MARKET THEFT. THE RE-OVENING OF A CASE.—Mr. Stuart applied on behalf of Mr. Abbott, builder, Chorley, for the re-opening of a case which was decided ai,ainst his client on the previous sessions day. He said that Mr. Abbott had been fined for allowing some property f his in Golborne to be tenanted before having been passed by the local surveyor, and he had not appeared when the case wag called. He had left Chorley that morning but had inissed his connection, on the way. He telegraphed to the court explaining the matter, but the telegram did not arrive until the business of the court was finished. His application was that the case should be restored and heard the next court day.—The bench ruled that the case could not be re-heard. INFRINGEMENT OF TRH BUILDING BYE-LAWS.—John Lea, Crow-lane, was summoned by the Newton Board of Improvement Commissioners in two instances for c-an- miffing breaches of bye-laws 31 and 42 between the 31st Oct. aryl 15th November, by (1) connecting certain drain pires in a contrary manner to that approved by the Board,and (2) by neglecting to comply with a notice signed by the Commissioners' Surveyor requiring him (defendant) to do the work in question properly.—Mr. J. E. Worsley, law clerk to the Commissioners, prose- outed, and Mr. Stuart, solicitor, defended.—ln openinm his case Mr. Woreley stated that the hearing had been adjourned from the last court day in order that the summsms might be amended, Mr Stuart having on that occasion, taken objection to a portion cf it. He (Mr. Woraley) had hoped that, during the interval, the work would have been done by the defendant, but that wa, not the case, and the hearing would have, consequently to proceed. The defendant had deliberately committed a violation of the bye-laws cited, and if the obj-ctions raised against the case were defeated then Mr. Worsley hoped that the bench would inflict as heavy a penalty as the legislature allowed. The defendant had recently erected property at r Earleatown, but before doing so submitted plans, showing the proposed drainage system to the Board of Commissioners. Those plans met the re- quirements of the members and were approved. Having concluded the drains it was the duty ofthe defendant to have called in the Board's Surveyor. Mr. R. Brierlev, so that that gentleman might have inspected them. The defendant should then have obtain,cl a certificate from the Board that everything had been properly done. He had not done anything of the sort, however, but had covered the drains up without giving the least notice. Mr. Brierley subsequently had the drains unearthed and found them to be insanitary and dangerous to health. Mr. Brierley afterwards saw defendant and gave him 48 hours in which to do the work perfectly, but the work not having been done,these proceedings had been tßken. —Mr. Richard Brierley, clerk and surveyor to the Board gave evidence relating to the fault; drainage, and in answer to Mr. Stuart expressed it as his opinion that the defendant had probably saved £4 or £5 through Putting smaller and fewer pipes down for drainage pur- posea.--Mr. Stuart, for the defence, thought that the offence was not at all a serious one. His client had felt sore because he was forced to put down six-inch pipes when only four inch pipes had been put down nn certain property near to that which defendant had erected, and vet nothing had been said to the builder in this case. He held that his client could net be convicted for contra- veninr, bye-law 42 because no notice of these proceedings was given to him.—Defendant was fined 403. and code.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON A. v. STOCKPORT A. The first team had no fixture on Saturday, and con- sequently the A team had the day to themselves. They bed the previous week been defeated by Stockport A, and as they again met on Saturday they were deter- mined to wipe out the score with interest. The wind and the weather generally, however, spoiled their chance of rubbing it into the Stockport men, and they only managed to win by a bare in of a try to nil. The time announced for the kick off was 2.45, but it was about 3.25 betore the match began. Just before the players eritered the field, a severe storm burst over the field, but fortunately it cleared up before acei ve operations commenced, and it kept fair until near the end of the match,al- hough old Boreae was having a rare innings to himself all the time, and pleyed some queer pranks with the hall There was only a small attend- ance, which is not to be wondered at considerine the weather. He must be an enthusiast indeed who faced the storm on Saturday to see the game. Those whe were not on the grand stand, and had to bear the brunt of the storm which came on towards' the end of the game were sincerely to be pitied. Towards 3.25 thirty oold-looking players came on the ground to commence ' operations under tee direction of eh. Berry, of Fails- worth, and the following was the composition of the teams :_Warrington : Full baok, Boscow ; three quar- ters, Lawless, Appleton, Thornton, and Forster ; half backs, Bennett and Forster; forwards, Randalls, Hill, Burton, Houghton, Freece, Saunders, Swift, and Barrett. Stockport : Full back, Haslarn ; three-quarters, Brown, Sands, Alcock, and Hasnett : half hacks, Heaths cote and Goaclall ; forwards, Mills, Leavy, Hay- ward, Miller, Walker, Chaeiburn, and Stafford. Stockport were fortunate in winning the toes, and , during the whole of the first half they had the assistance , of a very strong wind. Burton, who it will be noticed, played in the forwards, kicked off for Warrington to- wards the pavilion goal, and the ball being poorly re- turned, Warrington were quickly on the way to the ' visitors' lines,but found a stumeiing block in Haslam,the old Cheshire and Yerk4hire county player, who by a good kick found touch at half-way. From the subse- quent fine cut, Warrington again made tracks for the Stockport goal, but too much eagerness spoiled the effort, and only a minor resulted. Warrington were re- peatedly within an ace of scoring in the teeth of the gale, and had very hard lines indeed. At leneth from a general melee, Lawless secured, and d dein !lAlcoele,ra,n fre;ne the quarter line closely followed by Alcock and Heenett, but he succeeded in grounding Just its time. Lawless made a fine attempt at goal, but the wind blew the ball just outside. Up te this point Stockport had never been over the half wee, It ne,but now they breke a- way with a splendid dribble,and succeeded in getting the ball over the Warrington line, but Biscow was very alert, and touched down. This was the last effort of Stockport this half, and Warrington were just prevented from scoring by Hasler-a making his mark when sur- rounded by the Warrington forwards. Ws kick sent the ball well ,tit of danger and half time soon after- wards arrived with the score of a try to nil in favour' of Werrington. Mills kieked off after the interval, and it was generaely expected that with the wind Warrington would carry all before them, hut for a short time Ssockpert more than held their own, amd almost equalised several times. War- rington at length got away by a forward rush, and during the rest of the game they were continually press- ing. Burton had now gone half back, and B4scoW who had nothing to do as full back,appearrsd to have a roving commission, and was to be seen continually in the thick of the fray. The severe weather, however, completely spoiled the play,and after about ten minutes play in this half, a heavy hailstone came down with terrific forte and oompletely finished up the players. They played on to the end in a sort of lackadaisical, care for nothing manner, and they were very glad when the whistle sounded for full:time with the score still standing at oRe try to none for Warrington. LYMII URB IN DIbTRICT COUNCIL.

The first meetin! of this Council was held in the Board-room on Monday night. There were present— Dr. Williamson, ant Messrs. J. R. Smith, E. Brown, W. A. Dewhurst, S. A.mlecon, S W. Gould, Edwin Holt, G. L. Welford. J. Grevon, and A. Mclntyre, Ridgway (law ,lerk), and Milliard (clerk). Mr. Appleton proposed Mr. Brown as provisional chairman, to take the vote on the appointment of chairman.—Mr. Smith seconded.—Mr. Ridgway : I do not see what you are driving at in that rosolution, as the provisional chairman will not have a casting vote.—Mr. Welford I think he has.—Mr. Holt thought it had nothing to do with it.—Mr. Brown was elacted unanimously.

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN. Mr. Brown then took the chair, and said the first business was to apnoint a chairman, to take office until the 15th April, 1895.—Dr. Williamson.—Before we take the vote I should like to move a rosolction that the' chairman elected shall have a majority of votes of the members preEmt.—Mr. Smith seconded the resolution. —Mr. Gregson moved an amendment to the effect that Vie one who got the highest number of votes indepen- dent of the members present be elected.—Mr. Dew- hurst seconded.—On the amendment being put, five voted for it, and .five for the resolution.— Mr. Wedford : I think Mr. Brown has a casting vote.— Mr. Ridgway : As the Chairman is not the chairman of the Council, he has no casting vote. I can quite see that Mr. Brown's position has not been contemplated at all, and, therefore you cannot go outside the four corners of the Act.—Mr. Welford : I cannot remember where! have road it, but I think it is somewhere stated that the provisional chairman has a casting vote.—Mr. Ridgway : You are doing something not contemplated by the Act. When you get a chairman appointed you can deal with the business as it arises.—Mr. Brown : Caneot I claim the full powers of the chairman P—Mr. Flolt : That would create a minority into a majority.— Mr. Smite said he had been a member of Manchester Corporate bodies for a number of years, and bad never known it otherwise than the chairman having a casting vote.—Mr. Ridgway : I think you are out of order ;if one man gets three votes, another three, and another four, he with the four would have the majority of the votes.—Matters had come to such a deadlock that Mr. Ridgway suggested that the officials and the reporters leave the room, and that the members have a trial vote as to whe should be chairman, so that when the re- porters came bank it could be put and carried. The reporters and officials then withdrew' and on being re- admitted, a few minutes afterwards, Mr. Dewhurst pro- posed t kat Mr. Gregson be the chairman until the 15th April,lB9s.—Mr. Gould seconded, and on being put the following voted for it :—Aessrs. Gould, [Felt, Dewhurst, Williameon, Appleton, and Gregson.—The resolution was then declared carried.

ELECTION Or VICE-CHAIRMAN. Mr. Smith moved that they have a vice-chairman.-- Mr. Gould seconded, and it was carried.—Mr. Holt then proposed Mr. Welford as vice-chairman.—Mr. Appleton s.ronded, and it was carried.—Mr. WelfGrd briefly replied, and said he would try and assist Mr. Gregson in any way he could. THE APPOINTMENT Or COMMITTEES.

The following committees were appointed :—Building and Fire Committee : Messrs. Appleton, Gould and Holt. Finance Committee: Messrs. Brown, Dewhurst, Smith and Holt. Gas Committee : 'Messrs. Dewhurst, Holt, Mentyre and Williamson. Roads Committee : Messrs. Smith. Gould, Mclntyre, and Dewhurst. Sani- tary Committee : Messrs. Brown, Appleton, Smith and Williamson. School Attendance Committee: Messrs. Brown, Williamson and Welford. Sewage Farm Com- mittee : Messrs. Appleton, Smith. Gould and Mclntyre. On the motion of Mr. Appleton, seconded by Mr. Gould, it was decided that the chairman and vice-chair- , man of the Council be ex officio members of each com- mittee.

MISCELLANZOIIS.

rust then. Mr. Smith : Am I in order in asking whether we Rh ou Id have the office of the Local Board open P—Mr. Ridgway : You cmn discuss anything.—Mr. Smith : Can we not have the office open a few hours daily P—The Chairman : We shall need a resolution.—Mr. Brown :It is very hard on people coming from a distance to find the office closed.—Mr. Welford : Don't you think it would be best to give notice of motion that the matter he discussed at: the next meeting ?—Mr. Smith then gay. notice that h would move at the next meeting that the office he open daily. A LAND OF SHIM' CONTRASTS.

-- What a land of sharp and terrible contrasts is the great Republic of the United States! At no timc in the year more than the present do misery and hi *ger pervades Chicago, and yet, sat s the " Daily Chronicre,'' nowhere, perhaps, in the American Union 14 the ostentation of more wealth se great. A few d ays ago a railway train composed of fifteen carriages, which cost nearly £1.00.000 to build, left that city with a few families,who were journeying to a wedding at Burlington, Iowa; and the heads of families in the train repre.ented fortune exceeding in the aggregate £100.000.000 Never has such a luxurious train or so wealthy a body of passed gers, it is said, travelled over a Western railway since railways had existence. Then the president of one rail- way went to the wedding in his own railway carriages described as a perfect palace on wheels; the president of tfie Western Union Telegraph Company WAS there and with his new £lO,OOO railway carriage; and the officials of the Pullman Company travelled in a gorgeous special carriage. With music and feasting, surrounded by every form of luxury, this handful of millionaires swept out of a city where hunger held high carnival ; swept over th., prairies where the farmers, debt crushed and monopoly robbed, were brooding over the year's black record ; On to the marriage feast at the palatial house of one of their own millionaire class.

HAYDOCK.

Tiro w YEAR.—The ushering in of the New Year was carried out in a rein trkably quiet manner. Watch services were held on New. Year's Eve at the different places of worship, and were very well attended. Work was suspended .at the collieries during the week until Thursday marning. Oa New Year's night a dance was held at the new schoolroom attached to the St. Alban's mission, a fair number tviing present. GENERAL NEWS.

--- A he• broke out at an earls' hour on Wednesday morn- ing in a laundry owned by Madame Mirtin in Edgware road, London. It spread with appalling rapidity. Eleven fire engines were quickly in attendance, hut all the firemen could do was to keep the flames from spre +d- ing to the adjoining buildings. Five charred bodies were afterwards found in the ruins, and it is stated that at least three other persons have been burned to death. According to a telegram frcon St. Petersburg, the Russian Government, desiro is of sf Ming the Pamir question amicably, have made great concessions to Great Britain.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

Stringer, in the course of his address, spoke of the lessons to be learned by the events of the pa...3l,year. The Rev. J. ICirtlan also spoke in a similar strain. Immediately after twelve o'clock, the choir and congregation jlined in the hymn, "Come let us anew," after which Mr. Kirtlan wished all present "A Happy New Year." The congregation then separated.

THE PARISH CHURCH.

There was a large congregation at the Watcbnight Service at tho Parish Church. All the clergy were present, namely, the Rector (the Rev. F. W. Willis), the Revs. G. B. Thurston, A. A. Markham, and H. M. R. Hutt. A hymn having been sung anclthe collects read, the Rev. G. B. Thurston gave an address which lasted until two minutes to twelve. The congregation were then asked to engAge in silent prayer until twelve. Directly the clock chimed the midnight hour, the con- gregation ieined in singing "All hail the power of Jesu'4 name," after which the Rector gave his New Year's greeting. The singing of another hymn concluded the service.

Nor is ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The weather on Sat urday was a repetition on a smaller scale of the previoui Saturday, but in addition to the wind, there was also a sample of all kinds of weather, such as hail, sleet, rain, snow, and goodness knows what. In tact so bar' was the afternoon, that the gates were not opened for the public until a quarter of an hour alter the time advertiged for the kick off. When the gates were opened, a smsll crowd of shivering, chattering specimens of humanity got through, and took their pl ices round the " ropes," with an air of resigna- tion. And they needed all the resignation they possessed too, for during the course of the match, a storm which almost equall,d a bUzzi.rd came on, in the midst of which, the players could scarcely be distinguished.

The first team were not sorry that they had no match on that day, although a few of them did not have the rest which they were entitled to, for Boscow, Burton, and Houghton, came to assist the A team to wipe off that little score which was made against them the pre- vious Saturday, by the Stockport A. The Stockport men arrived in Warrneton at two o'clock, hut when they arrived on the field, they did not feel particularly anxious to comme Ice the aavne. The prospect cer- tainly was not very cheering. The fl-Id was almost a quagmire, and a strong gale was blowing, which would certainly find out all the soft places in them. However, about three qnart,,ra of an hour late the match began. but only two twenties were played.

Under the circumstances, it is not surprising that the game was a complete fiasco. Neither side put that earnestness into their play which spells success. So long as the ball was k .pt clear of their lines, the players did not appear to care very much. Warrington however had by far the best of what little play there was. Ex- cept or' two occasions, even against the wind, the game was either on the halfway line, or in the Stockport quar- ters. On only one occasion was the ball taken over,and that was by Lawless, who thus scored the winning point.

To come to the plovers. Well it would not be fair to criticise them. Suffice it to say that the half backs and three-quarters did not di.mlay much dash, with the ex- ception of LaWio49. Burton Tsurprised everybody by going among the forwards, and his long fcrin was very prominent in the scrimmages. He mado some. very dangerous runs, and with a little luck he would have been over at least twice. Be played half back in the second half so that he was of a rather rainb!ina dis- position during the match. It was among the forwards that Warrington held the upper hand in the match. They continually beat the Stockport men, and had the weather permitted the backs to take advantage of their superiority, ' a good score would undoubtedly have been made.

If Burton was enjoying himself, what must be said of Boscow ? He is generally supposed to be the full bark, but as he had nothing to do there, he determined to find something to keep himself warm. He was, therefore, at tim.a to be seen among the three-quarters. Then he was to he seen with his hack bent watching the , ball in the scrimmage, and then at line outs he could be 'seen among the forwards. Fie had truly a roving corn- misgiwi on Saturday. The fact that the full back could do this witheut endangering his side shows that War- rington were much the superior of the other side, for Raglan.' for Stockport had plenty of work to do in his own department, and lie did that well.

There should have been two matches on New Year',i Day, the first te‘m v. Kendal Hornets, and the A v. Widnes, but they were both cancelled owing to the frost,which had made the ground too hard to play. The first-mentioned instch would have produced a good gate, althou2h the Hornets are not the team they once were. In the other match, the Warrington A team were disappointed of not having the chance of wiping off some old scores. Three times in succession the Widnes A team defe.ited them, and therefore it was time that some turn was made the other way.

To-day Warrington travel all the way to Tilverston-in- Furness to meet the team of that town. It is some years since the teams have met previously, and, therefore, it would be difficult to compare them. The matches were usually of a close nature, and as Ulverston are the only team which have up to the present time beaten Widnes in the second league, a good game is looked forward to by the Warrington men.

To meet them the Warrington Committee have picked the following team :—Floscow, full back ; Barber, Moores. IFleesom, and Burton. three-quarter bleka ; Bate and Nevins, half backs ; Turner, Jolley, Houghton, Savage, Barrett, Taylor, Dakin, and Bailey, forwards.

The chief football event of to-day is the match be- tween England and Wales. For England the whole of the team which represented the South has been chosen— with the exception of Wells, who is replaced by Taylor, and thus the whole of the Yorkshire men are exclu&d. .11owever,it is raid several of the team have cried off,and thus several Tykes will be in the team alter all. Tho Yorkshire fosthalf enthusiasts will, therefore, have some little interest in the match. Wales fancy themselves this year, I believe, and it would not be the least sur- prising to see them give England a good beating. It ; at any rate not expected that England will run up RI( a score as she did last year at Birkenhead.

THE RELIGION OF JAPAN.

It would be hard to say just what the present religion of the Japanese is. There are all kinds of sects, Bud- dhism probably predominating. Since the Mikado re- nounced bis infallible and sacred character considerable looseness in religious thought or superstitios prevails— leaving, of course all the more room for the advance of Christianity. The temples are very simple structures, the chief internal feature in many of them being a look- ing-glass, supposed to be emblematic of the soul's purity. Near, it is a font containing water. In this the worshipper washes, then proceeds to the looking-glass and prays for what he wants, and after leaving a few coppers in a box near by, rings a bell at the door thrice as he goes out.

===== 0002601_18950105_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. T. M. HEALY AND THE LIBERAL

PARTY.

Mr. T. M. Healy, M.P., speaking on Sunday at a demonstration at Sixmilebridge, deprecated the policy of the Redmondite party in endeavouring to throw the present Government out of office. Be i,xpected good from Mr. Morley's coming Land Bill, and believed in the integrity of the vast body of the Liberal party and in the Liberal Cabinet Signs there might be in one or two places of lagging or of weakening, but it would be for:the Liberals to give evidence to show that these signs represented no widespread disease or disaffection in their party; but were the results of the utterances of cranks or faint hearts.

WAS THIS MAN INSANE?

The reflections suggested by one of the latest crimes committed in the light of day in a frequent A thorough- fare of the French capital are quite the reverse of re- ' assuring. A Russian doctor named Mein, a resident in Paris, was walking along the 13,mlevard Sebastopol early in the morning, when a man passed him whistling I merrily; but,as the unexpected sequel proved.whistling, unfortunately for himself,', out of tune. The Russian doctor, whose nerves apparently were easily irritated, became exasperated beyond, measure at the unbarmoni- ous sounds, and, pulling a revolver out of his pocket, he lodged three bullets in the person of the inoffensive musician, an honest tradesman. This, says the " Evening Standard," was distinctly a most reprehen- sible manner of impressing on the poor fellow the de- fective nature of his musical education, and it is gratify- ing to know that the brutal aesailant was arrested on the spot. The more so, seeing that on being searched at the police-station he was found to be provided with a dagger, a sword-stick, and a knuckle-duster, in addition to the revolver, a formidable arsenal, the possession of which he explained by the observation that there were more ruffians in the world than honest men, so it was necessary to be always well armed. Now that he him- self is under lock and key, there will, at all events for a peried, be one ruffian the less at large.

" JOBE-MAKING " IN AMERICA.

The American "joke-making trade" is described by Mr. Joseph Hatton. Its chief centre is in New York. There is a coterie of about forty writes. They do nothing but write for the comic papers. It is not th”ir habit to wait for inspiration. They compel the humorous Muse to work with them in a regular and systematic way. On an average they earn from forty to sixty pounds a month. One man will turn out as many as from eighty to a hundred jokes a week. Every one of the squibs is warranted brand new, original and imperishable. The joker sits down to his desk; some odd turn of speech or nappy thought comes to him.. Ile jots it down on a slip of paper convenient for an envelope. One good idea suggests another, and " copy " grows. When he has accumulated five-and-twenty he entsrs them in a honk with the names of the papers to which they are sent and awaits the issue. But first, be it under- stood, he sends his packet to the paper that pays the highest prices, and so on down to the lowest until the batch is exhausted. The lowest financial depths represent from sevenpmce to a shilling for a joke, but the best pap-frs pay liberally. He checke off the jokes as they are accepto,i, and works through his stock-in-trade from week to week. A few jokers are on the staffs of leading comic payers, but the rule is for jokers to he more or less unattached, and thus the merry columns' of over five-and-twenty good-paving humorous New Y Irk papers are sure of a plentiful supply of fun. It is interesting to follow a joke on its ravels. I need hardly say that I an not u humorist ' • but I have invented a jolt-, or two in my time, and dramatised many an unrehearsed incident of our daily life. i literary father well knows the offspring of his pen, and I have seen mine come up fresh and solling at home again alter travelling for years through the columns of American and Colonial papers and one in a way having the privilege of a kindly reception even on the Continent. There is msnv a chestnut that merits our heartiest res- pect ; " I know," a s :Mark w airi woul3 say, " because =ave been there."

WARIIINOTON

tetterss to the Otittor.

_ THE CONDITION OF RUNCORN.

Sir,—The illustrious body of commissioners has departed from this stage of existence after a long and tedious illness called creek and water diabetes. A new and, let us hope, a better system has been inaugurated, called the District Urban Council. May they raise the creek to a seaport and so brit': about a revival in the shipping trade, and also improve what at times seems a a ater famine, for how often do we go to our taps for water but behold instead of water there is nothing but, as it were, a hissing night wind. Wait we must for water, it seems, but not for water rates—those have to be paid in advance. May we under a new government have found for us, a more constant supply, or at least, receive timely notice that the water will tie shut off so that a stock may be obtained by the pap.rs for such water. Then about the unemployed. Could not the D.U.C., as in other places, start some at least of the new streets that are to be made, not by contracting, but for themselves, so finding relief work for the scores of men and youths now out of work. These could be employed in wheeling, to and from, sueh material as is required. It is said men would not do it. Well, try them. I feel sure all men would prefer to receive 3s. 6d. or 44. per day (youths something less) for such work than either relief tickets or employment at Stone•breaking. If the unemployed could earn some wages however little, it would circulate the money, and parents and children would not be as at present starv- ing. This would enable shopkeepers and others to keep the wolf from the door, and, in fact, all would Irre helped ; yes, even the rate collectors. This relief work would be no additional expense on the ratepayers, as the owners of property have to pay the estimated cost. Let us hope our selection in the late election has been a wise one, and that tte D.U.C. will set these matters go- ing as their first great work.—Yours truly, ISAAC BOW YER.

48, Church-St., Runcorn. ---..-.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. The annual tea meeting in connection with the Bold- st. Wesleyan Church was held in the Bold-3t. Wesleyan Schoolroom, on Tuesday, when an excellent tea was partaken of by 150 members of the congregation. The tables were presided over by lady members of the con- gregation. After the annual tea an entertainment and meeting, presided ever by Rev.J. Kirtlan. was given in the school- room. 1 here was a good attendance and'at the com- mencement the Chairman called upon Mr. J. Lingard (secretary), to read his annual report of the committee. In the course of the report Mr. Lingard said : "The , committee have pleasure in stating that the numbers ' have been well sustained, and will compare favourably with the past or previous years. Some of the cla es•-s have largely increased,and especially among the younger scholars. The Band of Hope has a membership of 250 and weekly meetings are held in the Band room but the average attendance is rather small, a few more earnest workets would be heartily welcomed. The juvenile missionary collectors have collected about £34 during the past year, great praise is due to them and Mr. Jubb the secretary for their services rendered in ' the past and hope t'aey will be encouraged to go forward in the future. The library is composed ef 420 very interesting and instruc- tive books which are read by a large number of our scholars. It is with regret we receive the reAgnation of Mr. Dunn who has attended so fa.ithfolly to his duties for a number of years. We hope he will be of great use to the church in other ways, and that our newly ap- pointed assistant, along with Mr. Boclie, will be con- nected with the school the same length of time and will carry out his duties as faithfully as his predecessor. We must not forget to thank Mr. Mellor who has been connected with the school as secretary for six years. The duties of the secretary are very numerous, those only who have held the offiee, can estimate the time that is required to be given to the many details of a school like this. We thank the teachers and all the officers of the school for the kind interest they mani- f:st in the school Sunday by Sunday, and Mr. Silcock, our respected superintendent, who is so attentive and punctual,the right man in the right place.TheY.P.S C.E. must not be forgotten,:for this is composed of a large number of our young people, and is doing a grand work. The Societa 's great aim is to get the members to work and to live for Christ. He trusted that that meeting would be the means of deepening the interest of everyone in the congregation of their church. The teachers had worked most heartily for their classes, but it was most desirable that they should encourage their friends to begin the new year, next Sunday morn- ing, by being present, and to make up their minds to be there early, every morning in the year. He hoped that all would endeavour to make their Sunday school the most prosperous in the circuit. It had been arranged that a Sunday School Union should he formed with a view of encouraging their friends in their work and also with a view of bringing all the schools in the circuit up to the level of theirs. The room was seasonably decorated with evergreens, bunting and Chinese lanterns, and had a very attractive appearance.

WARRINGTON.

BALL AT CAIRO-STREET ECHOOLS. On Monday night a grand ball was held in the abate schools, there being about 60 present, and the dancing was kept up with spirit until about one o'clock. Mr. A. C. Brooks officiated as M.C. Dur- ing the evening several songs were rendered by Mr. J. Emson and Mr. W. C. Brooks, which were well received by the audience. The pianist was Mr. C.

Broadhurst. TESTING THEIR TALENTS. Impromptu speeches were the feature of the last meeting of the Baptist Mutual Improvement Society held in the schoolroom, Golborne-street, on Friday evening week. Mr. W.Drewett presided over the gathering, which proved interesting in its sub- jects selected, and inttructive as to the progress made by the various speakers by the opportunities for public:speaking afforded them during the session. All the gentlemen and several of the lady members •-ayed the difficult task of expressing their opin- ions on promiscuous subjects off-hand, and some of the speakers showed the possession of the quali- ties requisite to make successful declaimers. The meeting closed the first session of the season,which has succeeded in the objects the promoters had in view in arousing and developing the latent talents of the young people connected with the Baptist ,Church. BANIC-ST. MISSION TEA. PARTY. The annual congregational tea party was held at Bank-st. Mission on Tuesday. Over 150 sat down to an excellent tea at half-past six, which was pre- sided over by Mrs. Willis, Mrs. Walton, Mrs. Collins, Mrs. Hodgson, Mrs. A. Woods, Mrs. Sanders, Miss Boardman, and many other ladies who lent their kind assistance. After tea the Rev. A. A. Markham (curate-in-charge) in a short speech, after referring to a few events of notice in the past year,expressed his cordial thanks to the men of the Bank-street Bible Class who had given their time and labour so as to convert the two cottages adjacent to the Mis- sion into a handsome workingmen's club. The pre- : raises were now connected with the Mission room and provided much extra space and accommoda- tion. The club rooms were to be inaugurated that .evening, and he trusted that many workingmen of the district would take advantage of them, and he rejoiced that the result of the voluntary work on the part of the members of the Bible Class would be that the Mission would be a greater power for good in that neighbourhood. After the tables were cleared an excellent entertainment was provided by Mr. F. J. Holland, of Liverpool, who amused the audience by his clever conjuring tricks and magical illusions. The proceedings terminated with a dance. BODY FOUND IN THE RIVE/I.—INQUEST Mr. Brighouse held an inquest on Monday at the Police Stet ion,on the body of John Woollams.which was found drowned in the River Mersey on Friday morning. Sarah Woollams, Failsworth, near Old- ham, said that deceased was her husband, and was n years of age, and a bricksetter by trade. lie left home three weeks previously at 6.30 a.m., and she did not see him again alive. Be had left her with- out any means.—Wm. Smith said that a fortnight the previous Monday, he and deceased left Fails- worth to go "on the spree." They arrived in War- rington the.previous Thursday night and lodged in Rose and Crown-st... for the night. The deceased was very restless during the night, and early on Friday morning, he went out presumably to get. some drink. The next he heard of him was that deceased's body had been found in the river. They had had plenty of ale during the time they had been away from home —John Dunbabin gave evidetwe to pulling the body out of the river near Fairclongh's flour mills. The jusy returned a ver- dict of " Found drowned," and the coroner consider- ing the straitened circumstances of the widow allowed her 10s. out of the poor box. SERVICES FOR THE PEOPLE AT BOLD-ST On Sunday afternoon the usual people's service was held in the Bold-st. Wesleyan Church, the Bev. G. D. Stetson presiding. After a selection of music had been played by the orchestral band,and a hymn had been sung, Mr. A. ti. Wild read a portion of scripturs, and Mr. F. 0. Mo,ton (of Lymm) prayed An Andante by Tolhurst was then very pleasingly rendered as a violin solo by Miss Winnie Pigot, and another hymnihaving been sung, 'Mr. F. 0. Mnsten was called upon to give the address. He spoke of the great pleasure with which he had looked for- , ard to that afternoon, and took the opportunity of wishing them all a Happy New Year. He hoped that, the year 1895 would be the brightest, happiest, and most proeperous one they had ever hail. He thought he could tell them how they might all titmice it so. Let their mottabe " Heaven's light our guide," and he ventured to prophesy that the com- ing veer would be all that could he desired. He reminded them that life was &journey, divided into stages, and tit the end of stage 1891, before entering upon stage 1895, they had a favourable oprortnnity of taking a retrospective glance over the portion of tile road already traversed. In doing this they would doubtless call to mind many who had gone astray by taking the wrong turn. It was necessary that they should each have a guide to conduct them safely through the journey of life. Some people api-ilte of reason and intellect as guide, ; it was a ' great mistake, for these, he said, would only lead them into the middle of the forest of life's difficul- ties, and there would leave them unable to render any further assistance. The Bible, which was the guide he advocated, would lead them safely through the storm as well as through the calm, through sorrow as well as through joy, through cloud as well as through sunshine to the end of life. " Lead, kindly light," by Gmmod, was then feelingly sung by Miss Pod le, of London, and the orchestra played a selection from MriZirt's 12th Mass, during which a liberal response was made to the Chairman's ap• peal on behalf of the "Old folks at home," and in conolusion the congregation was further favoured by the hand playing a selestion from the "Sacred I Wreath."
