NEW YEAR'S EVE AND DAY AT EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

In striking and pleasing contrast to Christmas Eve, was the eve of the New Year. Although terribly cold, with a biting north wind, the weather was of a season- able character, and pedestrians had certainly a more en- joyable time in which to make whatever purchases were necessary, and also to visit their acquaintances. Towards midnight the streets became very animated, a large number of people making their way to the various places of worship, where watch night services were held. The viaduct brass band also turned out, and until the advent of the new year, played selections of music at various places in Eirlestown. The people who were about were very orderly, and the police had no trouble whatever, cases of drunkenness being conspicuously I absent. Whilst many people devoutly inclined were religiously waiting the With of the new year, a goodl eompany were enjoying themselves in the Mission B.iil,a dance being in progress under the auspices of the Earles- town Orchestral Society, and they did not separate until early on Tuesday morning. On the departure of the old year, cannons and rockets were fired, and numerous buzzers made a giscordant noise, whilst hearty greetings for a prosperous year, could be heard on every hand. At Newton the New Year was ushered in very quietly, and there were comparatively few people about. On Tuesday evening the various Sunday schools held their annual treats, and below will be found particulars of same.

BAPTIST SUNDAY SCBOOL. The annual tea and entertainment was held in the schoolroom on New Years D iy. 136 sat down, and their wants were ministered to by Mesdames Kenning,Morris and A. Limond, Messrs. P. J. and V. Partington, Limond, Cronin, Holt, S. and M. Fail and Morris, assisted by the patron, superi itendent, and male teachers. After tea the Rev. J. E. Miller presided, and a magic lantern entertainment was given in the chapel. illustrating Dickens's " Chimes." Mr. Hulme officiated as reader, and Mr. Morris had charge of the lantern. A number of comic views were also thrown upon the sheet. During an interval 77 books were distributed to the scholars as rewards for punctuality in attendance. Mr. W. Limond, secretary, also presented his report, in which he stated that the number of scholars on the books was 183, with an average attendance in the morn- ing of 108, afternoon 159 ; teachers and workers 21. Thirteen scholars had j, 'Med the church during the year. The Cnristian Endeavour Society, was again in a flourishing condition, the average attendance at the meetings being 40. The Band of Hope was still an important work, and was looked after by An earnest b idy of workers. He concluded his report by wishing all a happy and prosperous New Year. 'f he second part of the entertainment was then proceeded with, and con- sisted of a glee by the choir, songs by Misses Clare, F. Kenning, W. Fair, and Master A. Limond ; recitations by Master W. Hulme, Misses L. Hultne, M. Brindle, A. Scott, and dialogue by the scholars.

BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. The annual tea meeting was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom, when there were about 180 persons present the tables being presided over by the teachers and lady members of the congregation. An adjournment was afterwards made to the Town Hall, where an excellent concert was given before a large audience. The Rev. ,T. Bartley presided, and addressed a few seasonable re- marks to those present, enlarging upon the necessity for help from the parents in the tutoring of the scholars at the Sunday school. The programme was then pro- ceeded with and opened with an overture,"Le Chevalier Breton,"played in an efficient manner by the Earlestown Ore.hestral Society. The other instrumental pieces In- cluded the vocal waltz, "Ferryman !John," awl march, " Belphegor." in each of which, for a new Society, they acquitted themselves admirably. -- - THE PANTOMIME-- AT ST. HELENS.

The pantomime of " Dick Whittington" is having a most successful run at the new Theatre Royal and Opera House, St. Helens. New that the artistes have hot more accustomed to their parts their clever abilit;es are brought out to the full and the whole performance is gone through with remarkable smoothness. The crowded hoases which have assembled during the holidays ' have testified by their prolonged enthusiasm their hearty appreciation of the efforts of the genial proprietor Mr. Wallace RPvill, to provide a genuine entertainment for the festive season, and those who have not yet visited Mr. Revili's handsome Theatre should nvt miss the opportunity which is now afforded of three hours' fun and comicality. The Passmore Brothers give some extremely funmy per- formances and never fail to secure a well merited encore Messrs. Maurice and Ross, also do well, and indeed seem to work well together to keep the ball of fun roil- ing, In all they do, the local children are better than ever this year. They have been well trained and they render their songs and go through their dances and marches with an rase and grace which render their efforts very pleasing. If one may judge by the applause of the audience their performances are the feature of the production. Several new songs have been introduced,and as mentioned previously the scenery is very pretty, the colours blending beautifully with the gorgeous dresses of the artistes.

SAD DEATH OF A DISTRICT COUNCILLOR. The Widnes police on Wednesday received intel- ligence of the death of Arthur Thomas Johnson, 30 years of age, a farmer, of Heath House Farm, War- rington-road, Bold, near Widnes, who was found dead in a ditch at Bold, on Wednesday adorning. Mr. Johnson attended a sale at St. Helens on Tue 4- day, and returned to Bold by half-past five in the evi ning in company with another farmer named William Johnson,of Bold Old Hall Farm. The deceas- ed went to his friend's bouse,where he stayed until about half-past ten. Nothing further was seen of hiui until his body was found in the ditch which crosses the main road at Bold, by his wife,who was on her return home after being at Johnson's inquir- ing after him. The legs of the deceased were in the water, while his body lay on the side of the ditch. It is conjectured that while returning home,instead of crossing the bridge which covers the ditch, he walked between the end of the wall and the hedge, and thus fell into the ditch. The deceased was widely knovtn and greatly respected in Widnes, and much sympathy is felt for the bereaved wife. A fortnight ago he was elected district councillor for Bold. He leaves no children.

HEATH SIDE SCHOOLS, WARRINGTON.

These well known and popular schools were examined by H.M. Inspectors of Schools in- October last, and from the repot.; on them which has now been received by Mr. All. A. Jolley, correspondent, on behalf of the managers, it is satisfactory to learn that they continue to maintain their high character for usefulness and effi- ciency. The boys' school under the management of our old and esteemed townsman, Mr. D. W. Finney, has again won the highest possible awards in all the subjects —grammar, geography, singing, discinline, etc.—and also for general excellence, and is classed as "excellent." The girls' school gains the highest merit grant for grammar, singing from notes, needlework.cliscipline,and is classed as " good." The infants' school also does well and gains the award "good." A little over a thousand children were examined. A number of boys also do well in algebra as a specific. We may mention, too, that to script ore arid drawing is assigned an important place in the school routine, and that both of these subjects have been highly reported on I); the Dioccsan Inspector and the Science and Art Department respectively. But besides these things, the managers attach much importance to the moral training of the scholars, and also to hahits of oiclor, cleanliness, neatness, good behaviour, thrift, in- dustry, punctuality, &c. It may be said too, that while the Education Department is making strenuous efforts to place every school in the country in a thoroughly sanitary condition, the managers of these schools have, with wise folethought, anticipated these Departmental requirements, and have spared no expense in regard to cloak-rooms, !a7atorles, &c., in order to secure the health and comfort or all the scriolars, as well as the large staff et teachers in their service. Many of our readers are little aware what care and anxiety, and indeed what personal outlay, the responsibility of carrying on large schools like these brings t" busy managers, whose only reward is the gratitude of those who knot?, the value of the sacrifice they make for education. We are!sure, , therefore, that our readers generally, will extend to the managers of those schools, as well as to all others en , gaged in a similar good work a high meed of praise for tie spirit and determination with which they discharge 'heir onerous and self imposed work.

MARRIAGE OF M. JOHN POWNALL, OF RUNCORN. On Monday, the wed,ling took place of Mr. John Pownall, of Runcorn, to Miss Ethel Price, daughter of Mr. Thomas Price. of Cheltenham, at St. Mary's Church, Bootle. Mr. Wm. &dikes, of Runcorn, acted as best man,and Miss Manson was the brides- :maid. The happy pair occupy principal positions in Mrs. Gordon Hicks Opera Company which recently performed at Runcorn. They were the recipients of many handsome presents.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"WIFE," said a henpecked husband, "go to bed." "I won't ! " "Sib up, then. I will be obeyed somehow." "PUT me in the same room" (says an old philo- sopher) "with a number of young girls, and I will tell you, when the postman knocks—merely by watching the looks of some, and the actions of others—how many of them are in love." Sharpe (ditto) : Confound it! I wanted to bring him up as a vegetarian !" Thirteen of my corn- panions were capsized in a boat and found a watery grave." "And how is it you were not drowned ?' "I—l stayed at home." " Agent: "Excuse me, dear sir, but out of thost. qualities I generally contrive to make four matches." CHASTER, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 31st day of December instant, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. "." The ii aring of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddisbury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds; will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Jury for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 31st day of December instant, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Court House, Nether Koutsford, on Witiumsnev, the 2nd day of January I.ext, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon.
